L97: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 3
L100: Introduction
L103: These lecture notes give an overview of recent results in geometric Langlands correspondence which may yield applications to quantum field theory. It has long
L104: been suspected
L107: that the Langlands duality should somehow be related to various dualities observed in quantum field theory and string theory. Indeed, both the Langlands
L108: correspondence and the dualities in physics have emerged as some sort of non-abelian Fourier transforms.
L110: Moreover, the so-called Langlands dual group introduced by R. Langlands in \[1\] that is essential in the formulation of the Langlands correspondence also plays
L111:  a prominent role in the study of S-dualities in physics and was in fact also introduced by the physicists P. Goddard, J. Nuyts and D. Olive in the framework of
L112:  four-dimensional gauge theory \[2\]. In recent lectures \[3\] E. Witten outlined a possible scenario of how the two dualities – the Langlands duality and the
L113: S-duality – could be related to each other. It is based on a dimensional reduction of a four-dimensional gauge theory to two dimensions and the analysis of what
L114:  this reduction does to “D-branes”. In particular, Witten argued that the_t’Hooft operators of the four-dimensional gauge theory recently introduced by A.
L115: Kapustin \[4\] become, after the dimensional reduction, the Hecke operators that are essential ingredients of the Langlands correspondence. Thus, a_t’Hooft
L116: “eigenbrane” of the gauge theory becomes after the reduction a Hecke “eigensheaf”, an object of interest in the geometric Langlands correspondence. The work of
L117: Kapustin and Witten shows that the Langlands duality is indeed closely related to the S-duality of quantum field theory, and this opens up exciting
L118: possibilities for both subjects.
L120: The goal of these notes is two-fold: first, it is to give a motivated introduction to the Langlands Program, including its geometric reformulation, addressed
L121: primarily to physicists. I have tried to make it as self-contained as possible, requiring very little mathematical
L124: background. The second goal is to describe the connections between the Langlands
L127: Program and two-dimensional conformal field theory that have been found in the last few years. These connections give us important insights into the physical
L128: implications of the Langlands duality.
L130: The classical Langlands correspondence manifests a deep connection between number theory and representation theory. In particular, it relates subtle number
L131: theoretic data (such as the numbers of points of a mod p reduction of an elliptic curve defined by a cubic equation with integer coefficients) to more easily
L132: discernable data related to automorphic forms (such as the coefficients in the Fourier series expansion of a modular form on the upper half-plane). We will
L133: consider explicit examples of this relationship (having to do with the Taniyama-Shimura conjecture and Fermat’s last theorem) in Part I of this survey.
L135: So the origin of the Langlands Program is in number theory. Establishing the Langlands correspondence in this context has proved to be extremely hard. But
L136: number fields have close relatives called function fields, the fields of functions on algebraic curves defined over a finite field. The Langlands correspondence
L137:  has a counterpart for function fields, which is much better understood, and this will be the main subject of our interest in this survey.
L140: ### 4  EDWARD FRENKEL
L143: Function fields are defined geometrically (via algebraic curves), so one can use geometric intuition and geometric technique to elucidate the meaning of the
L144: Langlands correspondence. This is actually the primary reason why the correspondence is easier to understand
L147: in the function field context than in the number field context. Even more ambitiously, one can now try to switch from curves defined over finite fields to
L148: curves defined over the complex field – that is to Riemann surfaces. This requires a reformulation, called the geometric Langlands correspondence. This
L149: reformulation effectively puts the Langlands correspondence in the realm of complex algebraic geometry.
L151: Roughly speaking, the geometric Langlands correspondence predicts that to each rank n holomorphic vector bundle E with a holomorphic connection on a complex
L152: algebraic curve X one can attach an object called Hecke eigensheaf on the moduli space Bunn of rank n holomorphic vector bundles on X:
L155: holomorphic rank n bundles
L158: ### −→  Hecke eigensheaves on Bunn
L161: with connection on X
L164: A Hecke eigensheaf is a D-module on Bunn satisfying a certain property that is determined by E. More generally, if G is a complex reductive Lie group, and LG is
L165:  the
L168: Langlands dual group, then to a holomorphic LG-bundle with a holomorphic connection on X we should attach a Hecke eigensheaf on the moduli space BunG of
L169: holomorphic
L172: ### G-bundles on X:
L175: holomorphic LG-bundles
L178: ### −→  Hecke eigensheaves on BunG
L181: with connection on X
L184: I will give precise definitions of these objects in Part II of this survey.
L186: The main point is that we can use methods of two-dimensional conformal field theory to construct Hecke eigensheaves. Actually, the analogy between conformal
L187: field theory and the theory of automorphic representations was already observed a long time ago by E. Witten \[5\]. However, at that time the geometric
L188: Langlands correspondence had not yet been developed. As we will see, the geometric reformulation of the classical theory of automorphic representations will
L189: allow us to make the connection to conformal field theory more precise. To explain how this works, let us recall that chiral correlation functions in a
L190: (rational) conformal field theory \[6\] may be interpreted as sections of a holomorphic vector bundle on the moduli space of curves, equipped with a
L191: projectively flat connection \[7\]. The connection comes from the Ward identities expressing the variation of correlation functions under deformations of the
L192: complex structure on the underlying Riemann surface via the insertion in the correlation function of the stress tensor, which generates the Virasoro algebra
L193: symmetry of the theory. These bundles with projectively flat connection have been studied in the framework of Segal’s axioms of conformal field theory \[8\].
L194: Likewise, if we have a rational conformal field theory with affine Lie algebra symmetry \[9\], such as a Wess-Zumino-Witten (WZW) model \[10\], then conformal
L195: blocks give rise to sections of a holomorphic vector bundle with a projectively flat connection on the moduli space of G-bundles on X. The projectively flat
L196: connection comes from the Ward identities corresponding to the affine Lie algebra symmetry, which are expressed via the insertions of the currents generating an
L197:  affine Lie algebra, as I recall in Part III of this survey.
L200: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 5
L203: Now observe that the sheaf of holomorphic sections of a holomorphic vector bundle E over a manifold M with a holomorphic flat connection \nabla is the simplest
L204: example of a holonomic D-module on M. Indeed, we can multiply a section φ of E over an open subset U ⊂ M by any holomorphic function on U, and we can
L205: differentiate φ with respect to a holomorphic vector field ξ defined on U by using the connection operators: φ_7→ \nabla ξφ. Therefore we obtain an action of
L206: the sheaf of holomorphic differential operators on the sheaf of holomorphic sections of our bundle E. If \nabla is only projectively flat, then we obtain
L207: instead of a D-module what is called a twisted D-module. However, apart from bundles with a projectively flat connection, there exist other holonomic twisted
L208: D-modules. For example, a (holonomic) system of differential equations on M defines a (holonomic) Dmodule on M. If these equations have singularities on some
L209: divisors in M, then the
L212: sections of these D-module will also have singularities along those divisors (and nontrivial monodromies around those divisors), unlike the sections of just a
L213: plain bundle with
L216: connection.
L218: Applying the conformal blocks construction to a general conformal field theory, one obtains (twisted) D-modules on the moduli spaces of curves and bundles. In
L219: some conformal
L222: field theories, such as the WZW models, these D-module are bundles with projectively flat connections. But in other theories we obtain D-modules that are more
L223: sophisticated: for example, they may correspond to differential equations with singularities along divisors, as we will see below. It turns out that the Hecke
L224: eigensheaves that we are looking for can be obtained this way. The fact that they do not correspond to bundles with projectively flat connection is perhaps the
L225: main reason why these D-modules have, until now, not caught the attention of physicists.
L227: There are in fact at least two known scenarios in which the construction of conformal blocks gives rise to D-modules on BunG that are (at least conjecturally)
L228: the Hecke eigensheaves whose existence is predicted by the geometric Langlands correspondence. Let us
L231: briefly describe these two scenarios.
L233: In the first scenario we consider an affine Lie algebra at the critical level, k = −h ∨,
L236: ∨ is the dual Coxeter number. At the critical level the Segal-Sugawara current
L239: where h becomes commutative, and so we have a “conformal field theory” without a stress tensor. This may sound absurd to a physicist, but from the mathematical
L240: perspective this
L243: liability actually turns into an asset. Indeed, even though we do not have the Virasoro symmetry, we still have the affine Lie algebra symmetry, and so we can
L244: apply the conformal blocks construction to obtain a D-module on the moduli space of G-bundles on
L247: a Riemann surface X (though we cannot vary the complex structure on X). Moreover, because the Segal-Sugawara current is now commutative, we can force it to be
L248: equal to any numeric (or, as a physicist would say, “c-number”) projective connection on our curve X. So our “conformal field theory”, and hence the
L249: corresponding D-module, will depend on a continuous parameter: a projective connection on X.
L251: In the case of the affine Lie algebra bsl2 the Segal-Sugawara field generates the center of the chiral algebra of level_k = −2. For a general affine Lie algebra
L252:  bg, the center of the chiral algebra has \ell = rank g generating fields, and turns out to be canonically isomorphic to a classical limit of the W-algebra
L253: asociated to the Langlands dual group LG, as shown in \[11, 12\]. This isomorphism is obtained as a limit of a certain isomorphism of W-algebras that naturally
L254: arises in the context of T-duality of free bosonic theories compactified on
L257: ### 6  EDWARD FRENKEL
L260: tori. I will recall this construction below. So from this point of view the appearance of the Langlands dual group is directly linked to the T-duality of
L261: bosonic sigma models.
L263: The classical W-algebra of LG is the algebra of functions on the space of gauge equivalence classes of connections on the circle introduced originally by V.
L264: Drinfeld and V.
L267: Sokolov \[13\] in their study of the generalized KdV hierarchies. The Drinfeld-Sokolov construction has been recast in a more geometric way by A. Beilinson and
L268: V. Drinfeld, who called these gauge equivalence classes LG-opers \[14\]. For a general affine Lie algebra bg the procedure of equating the Segal-Sugawara
L269: current to a numeric projective connection becomes the procedure of equating the generating fields of the center to the components of a numeric LG-oper E on X.
L270: Thus, we obtain a family of “conformal field theories” depending on LG-opers on X, and we then take the corresponding D-modules of conformal
L273: blocks on the moduli space BunG of G-bundles on X.
L275: A marvelous result of A. Beilinson and V. Drinfeld \[15\] is that the D-module corresponding to a LG-oper E is nothing but the sought-after Hecke eigensheaf
L276: with “eigenvalue” E! Thus, “conformal field theory” of the critical level_k = −h ∨ solves the problem of constructing Hecke eigensheaves, at least for those
L277: LG-bundles with connection which admit
L280: the structure of a LG-oper (other flat LG-bundles can be dealt with similarly). This is explained in Part III of this survey.
L282: In the second scenario one considers a conformal field theory with affine Lie algebra
L285: ### ∨, so it is in some sense opposite to the
L288: symmetry of integral level_k that is less than −h traditional WZW model, where the level is a positive integer. In fact, theories with such values of level have
L289:  been considered by physicists in the framework of the WZW models corresponding to non-compact Lie groups, such as SL2(R) (they have many similarities to the
L290: Liouville theory, as was pointed out already in \[16\]). Beilinson and Drinfeld have defined explicitly an extended chiral algebra in such a theory, which they
L291: called the chiral Hecke algebra. In addition to the action of an affine Lie algebra bg, it carries an action of the Langlands dual group LG by symmetries. If G
L292: is abelian, then the chiral Hecke algebra is nothing but the chiral algebra of a free boson compactified on a torus. Using the LGsymmetry, we can “twist” this
L293: extended chiral algebra by any LG-bundle with connection E on our Riemann surface X, and so for each E we now obtain a particular chiral conformal field theory
L294: on X. Beilinson and Drinfeld have conjectured that the corresponding sheaf of conformal blocks on BunG is a Hecke eigensheaf with the “eigenvalue” E. I will not
L295:  discuss this construction in detail in this survey referring the reader instead to \[17\], Sect.
L297: 4.9, and \[18\] where the abelian case is considered and the reviews in \[19\] and \[20\], Sect.
L299: 20.5.These two examples show that the methods of two-dimensional conformal field theory are powerful and flexible enough to give us important examples of the
L300: geometric Langlands correspondence. This is the main message of this survey.
L302: These notes are split into three parts: the classical Langlands Program, its geometric reformulation, and the conformal field theory approach to the geometric
L303: Langlands correspondence. They may be read independently from each other, so a reader who is primarily
L306: interested in the geometric side of the story may jump ahead to Part II, and a reader who wants to know what conformal field theory has to do with this subject
L307: may very well start with Part III and later go back to Parts I and II to read about the origins of the Langlands Program.
L310: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 7
L313: Here is a more detailed description of the material presented in various parts.
L315: Part I gives an introduction to the classical Langlands correspondence. We start with some basic notions of number theory and then discuss the Langlands
L316: correspondence for number fields such as the field of rational numbers. I consider in detail a specific example which relates modular forms on the upper
L317: half-plane and two-dimensional representations of the Galois group coming from elliptic curves. This correspondence, known as the Taniyama-Shimura conjecture,
L318: is particularly important as it gives, among other things, the proof of Fermat’s last theorem. It is also a good illustration for the key ingredients of the
L319: Langlands correspondence. Next, we switch from number fields to function fields undescoring the similarities and differences between the two cases. I formulate
L320: more precisely the Langlands correspondence for function fields, which has been proved by V.
L322: Drinfeld and L. Lafforgue.
L324: Part II introduces the geometric reformulation of the Langlands correspondence. I tried to motivate every step of this reformulation and at the same time avoid
L325: the most difficult technical issues involved. In particular, I describe in detail the progression from functions to sheaves to perverse sheaves to D-modules, as
L326:  well as the link between automorphic representations and moduli spaces of bundles. I then formulate the geometric Langlands conjecture for GLn (following
L327: Drinfeld and Laumon) and discuss it in great detail in the abelian case_n = 1. This brings into the game some familiar geometric objects, such as the Jacobian,
L328: as well as the Fourier-Mukai transform. Next, we discuss the ingredients necessary for formulating the Langlands correspondence for arbitrary reductive groups. 
L329: In particular, we discuss in detail the affine Grassmannian, the Satake correspondence and its geometric version. At the end of Part II we speculate about a
L330: possible non-abelian extension of the Fourier-Mukai transform and its “quantum” deformation.
L332: Part III is devoted to the construction of Hecke eigensheaves in the framework of conformal field theory, following the work of Beilinson and Drinfeld \[15\]. I
L333:  start by recalling
L336: the notions of conformal blocks and bundles of conformal blocks in conformal field theories with affine Lie algebra symmetry, first as bundles (or sheaves) over
L337:  the moduli spaces of pointed Riemann surfaces and then over the moduli spaces of G-bundles. I discuss in detail the familiar example of the WZW models. Then I
L338: consider the center of the chiral algebra of an affine Lie algebra bg of critical level and its isomorphism with the classical W-algebra associated to the
L339: Langlands dual group LG following \[11, 12\]. I explain how this isomorphism arises in the context of T-duality. We then use this isomorphism to construct
L340: representations of bg attached to geometric objects called opers. The sheaves of coinvariants corresponding to these representations are the sought-after Hecke
L341: eigensheaves. I also discuss the connection with the Hitchin system and a generalization to
L344: more general flat LG-bundles, with and without ramification.
L346: Even in a long survey it is impossible to cover all essential aspects of the Langlands Program. To get a broader picture, I recommend the interested reader to
L347: consult the informative reviews \[21\]–\[27\]. My earlier review articles \[28, 29\] contain some of the material of the present notes in a more concise form as
L348:  well as additional topics not covered here.

L380: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 9
L383: ### Part I. The origins of the Langlands Program
L386: In the first part of this article I review the origins of the Langlands Program. We start by recalling some basic notions of number theory (Galois group,
L387: Frobenius elements, abelian class field theory). We then consider examples of the Langlands correspondence for the group GL2 over the rational ad\`eles. These
L388: examples relate in a surprising and non-trivial way modular forms on the upper half-plane and elliptic curves defined over rational numbers. Next, we recall the
L389:  analogy between number fields and function fields.
L391: In the context of function fields the Langlands correspondence has been established in the works of V. Drinfeld and L. Lafforgue. We give a pricise formulation
L392: of their results.
L395: 1. The Langlands correspondence over number fields
L398: 1.1. Galois group. Let us start by recalling some notions from number theory. A number field is by definition a finite extension of the field Q of rational
L399: numbers, i.e., a field
L402: containing Q which is a finite-dimensional vector space over Q. Such a field F is necessarily an algebraic extension of Q, obtained by adjoining to Q roots of
L403: polynomials with
L406: coefficients in Q. For example, the field
L409: \[
L411: Q(i) = {a + bi\|a \in Q,b \in Q}
L413: \]
L416: is obtained from Q by adjoining the roots of the polynomial x 2 + 1, denoted by i and −i.
L418: The coefficients of this polynomial are rational numbers, so the polynomial is defined over Q, but its roots are not. Therefore adjoining them to Q we obtain a
L419: larger field, which has dimension 2 as a vector space over Q.
L421: \[
L423: More generally, adjoining to Q a primitive Nth root of unity \zeta N we obtain the Nth cyclotomic field Q(\zeta N). Its dimension over Q is \varphi(N), the
L424: Euler function of N: the number of integers between_1 and N such that (m,N) = 1 (this notation means that_m is
L426: \]
L429: ### 2πi/N , but
L432: relatively prime to N). We can embed Q(ζN ) into C in such a way that ζN_7→ e this is not the only possible embedding of Q(ζN ) into C; we could also send ζN_7→
L433:  e 2πim/N , where (m,N) = 1.Suppose now that F is a number field, and let K be its finite extension, i.e., another field containing F, which has finite
L434: dimension as a vector space over F. This dimension is called the degree of this extension and is denoted by degF K. The group of all field automorphisms σ of K,
L435:  preserving the field structures and such that σ(x) = x for all x ∈ F, is called the Galois group of K/F and denoted by Gal(K/F). Note that if K′ is an
L436: extension of K, then any field automorphism of K′ will preserve K (although not pointwise), and so we have a natural homomorphism Gal(K′/F) → Gal(K/F). Its
L437: kernel is the normal subgroup of those elements that fix K pointwise, i.e., it is isomorphic to Gal(K′/K).
L439: For example, the Galois group Gal(Q(ζN )/Q) is naturally identified with the group
L442: \[
L444: (Z/NZ) \times = {[n] \in Z/NZ\|(n,N) = 1},
L446: \]
L449: | 10  EDWARD FRENKEL |  | 
L451: | --- | --- |
L453: \[
L455: | with respect to multiplication. The element [n] \in (Z/NZ) | \times gives rise to the automorphism of Q(\zeta N) sending \zeta N to \zeta |
L457: \]
L459: | n m | mn | 
L461: | N , and hence ζ N to ζ | N for all_m. If M divides N, | | then Q(ζM) is contained in Q(ζN ), and the corresponding homomorphism of the Galois | | | groups
L462: Gal(Q(ζN )/Q) → Gal(Q(ζM)/Q) coincides, under the above identification, with | |
L464: | the natural surjective homomorphism |  | 
L466: | × → (Z/MZ) pN,M : (Z/NZ) | ×, | 
L468: | sending [n] to [n] mod M. |  |
L471: The field obtained from F by adjoining the roots of all polynomials defined over F is called the algebraic closure of F and is denoted by F. Its group of
L472: symmetries is the Galois group Gal(F/F). Describing the structure of these Galois groups is one of the main questions of number theory.
L474: 1.2. Abelian class field theory. While at the moment we do not have a good description of the entire group Gal(F/F), it has been known for some time what is the
L475:  maximal abelian quotient of Gal(F/F) (i.e., the quotient by the commutator subgroup). This quotient is naturally identified with the Galois group of the
L476: maximal abelian extension F ab of F. By definition, F ab is the largest of all subfields of F whose Galois group is abelian.
L478: \[
L480: For F = Q, the classical Kronecker-Weber theorem says that the maximal abelian extension Qab is obtained by adjoining to Q all roots of unity. In other words,
L481: Qab is the | union of all cyclotomic fields Q(\zeta N) (where Q(\zeta M) is identified with the corresponding subfield of Q(\zeta N) for M dividing N).
L482: Therefore we obtain that the Galois group Gal(Qab/Q) | |
L484: \]
L486: | --- | --- |
L488: | is isomorphic to the inverse limit of the groups (Z/NZ) | × with respect to the system of | 
L490: | × → (Z/MZ) × for M dividing N: surjections pN,M : (Z/NZ) |  | 
L492: | (1.1) Gal(Q (Z/NZ) ab/Q) ≃ lim←− | ×. | 
L494: | By definition, an element of this inverse limit is a collection (xN ),N > 1, of elements of | | | (Z/NZ) × such that pN,M(xN ) = xM for all pairs N,M such 
L495: that M divides N. | |
L498: This inverse limit may be described more concretely using the notion of p-adic numbers.
L500: Recall (see, e.g., \[30\]) that if p is a prime, then a p-adic number is an infinite series of the form
L503: ### (1.2)  akp k + ak+1p k+1 + ak+2p k+2 + ... ,
L506: where each ak is an integer between_0 and p − 1, and we choose_k ∈ Z in such a way that ak_6= 0. One defines addition and multiplication of such expressions by
L507: “carrying” the result of powerwise addition and multiplication to the next power. One checks that with respect to these operations the p-adic numbers form a
L508: field denoted by Qp (for example, it is possible to find the inverse of each expression (1.2) by solving the obvious system of recurrence relations). It
L509: contains the subring Zp of p-adic integers which consists of the above expressions with_k ≥ 0. It is clear that Qp is the field of fractions of Zp. Note that
L510: the subring of Zp consisting of all finite series of the form (1.2) with_k ≥ 0 is just the ring of integers Z. The resulting embedding Z ֒→ Zp gives rise to the
L511:  embedding Q ֒→ Qp.

L708: For an arbitrary number field F one defines the ring AF of ad\`eles in a similar way. Like Q, any number field F has non-archimedian completions parameterized
L709: by prime ideals in its ring of integers OF . By definition, OF consists of all elements of F that are roots of monic polynomials with coefficients in F; monic
L710: means that the coefficient in front of the highest power is equal to 1. The corresponding norms on F are defined similarly to the p-adic norms, and the
L711: completions look like the fields of p-adic numbers (in fact, each of them is isomorphic to a finite extension of Qp for some p). There are also archimedian
L712: completions, which are isomorphic to either R or C, parameterized by the real and complex embeddings of F. The corresponding norms are obtained by taking the
L713: composition of an embedding of F into R or C and the standard norm on the latter.

L716: “ring of integers”, denoted by Ov, which is defined similarly to Zp. Now AF is defined as the restricted product of all (non-isomorphic) completions. Restricted
L717:  means that it consists of those collections of elements of Fv which belong to the ring of integers Ov ⊂ Fv for all but finitely many v’s corresponding to the

L761: 1.3. Frobenius automorphisms. Let us look at the extensions of the finite field of p elements Fp, where p is a prime. It is well-known that there is a unique,
L762: up to an isomorphism, extension of Fp of degree_n = 1, 2,... (see, e.g., \[30\]). It then has q = p_n elements and is denoted by Fq. The Galois group Gal(Fq/Fp)
L763:  is isomorphic to the cyclic group Z/nZ. A generator of this group is the Frobenius automorphism, which sends x ∈ Fq to x p ∈ Fq. It is clear from the binomial
L764: formula that this is indeed a field automorphism of Fq. Moreover, x p = x for all x ∈ Fp, so it preserves all elements of Fp. It is also not difficult to show
L765: that this automorphism has order exactly_n and that all automorphisms of Fq preserving Fp are its powers. Under the isomorphism Gal(Fq/Fp) ≃ Z/nZ the Frobenius
L766: automorphism goes to_1 mod_n. Observe that the field Fq may be included as a subfield of Fq ′ = q ′
L769: n
L772: ### ′ whenever q . The
L775: algebraic closure Fp of Fp is therefore the union of all fields Fq,q = p_n
L778: ### ,n > 0, with respect
L781: to this system of inclusions. Hence the Galois group Gal(Fp/Fp) is the inverse limit of the cyclic groups Z/nZ and hence is isomorphic to Zb introduced in
L782: formula (1.3).
L785: ### ′ = q ′
L788: Likewise, the Galois group Gal(Fq n

L801: the subgroup Z ⊂ Zb, of which Zb is a completion, and so it may be viewed as a topological generator of Zb. We will call it the Frobenius automorphism of Fq.
L803: Now, the main object of our interest is the Galois group Gal(F/F) for a number field F. Can relate this group to the Galois groups Gal(Fq/Fq)? It turns out that
L804:  the answer is yes. In fact, by making this connection, we will effectively transport the Frobenius automorphisms to Gal(F/F).
L806: Let us first look at a finite extension K of a number field F. Let v be a prime ideal in the ring of integers OF . The ring of integers OK contains OF and hence
L807:  v. The ideal (v) of OK generated by v splits as a product of prime ideals of OK. Let us pick one of them and denote it by w. Note that the residue field OF /v

L825: Define the decomposition group Dw of w as the subgroup of the Galois group Gal(K/F) of those elements σ that preserve the ideal w, i.e., such that for any x ∈ w
L826:  we have σ(x) ∈ w. Since any element of Gal(K/F) preserves F, and hence the ideal v of F, we obtain a natural homomorphism Dw → Gal(OK/w, OL/v). One can show
L827: that this homomorphism is surjective. The inertia group Iw of w is by definition the kernel of this homomorphism. The extension K/F is called unramified at v if
L828:  Iw = {1}. If this is the case, then we have

L1079: We will pursue a different direction. Instead of talking about the structure of the Galois group itself, we will look at its finite-dimensional representations.
L1080:  Note that for any group G, the one-dimensional representations of G are the same as those of its maximal abelian quotient. Moreover, one can obtain complete
L1081: information about the maximal abelian quotient of a group by considering its one-dimensional representations.
L1083: Therefore describing the maximal abelian quotient of Gal(F/F) is equivalent to describing one-dimensional representations of Gal(F/F). Thus, the above statement
L1084:  of

L1088: representations

L1118: A marvelous insight of Robert Langlands was to conjecture, in a letter to A. Weil \[31\] and in \[1\], that there exists a similar description of_n-dimensional
L1119: representations of Gal(F/F). Namely, he proposed that those should be related to irreducible representations of the group GLn(AF ) which occur in the space of
L1120: functions on the quotient
L1123: GLn(F)\\GLn(AF ). Such representations are called automorphic. 9 Schematically,
L1126: ### n-dimensional representations representations of GLn(AF )
L1129: −→ in functions on GLn(F)\\GLn(AF )
L1132: of Gal(F/F)
L1135: This relation and its generalizations are examples of what we now call the Langlands correspondence.
L1137: There are many reasons to believe that Langlands correspondence is a good way to tackle non-abelian Galois groups. First of all, according to the “Tannakian
L1138: philosophy”, one can reconstruct a group from the category of its finite-dimensional representations, equipped with the structure of the tensor product.
L1139: Therefore looking at the equivalence classes of n-dimensional representations of the Galois group may be viewed as a first step towards understanding its
L1140: structure.
L1142: Perhaps, even more importantly, one finds many interesting representations of Galois groups in “nature”. For example, the group Gal(Q/Q) will act on the
L1143: geometric invariants (such as the ´etale cohomologies) of an algebraic variety defined over Q. Thus, if we take
L1146: 8The word “essentially” is added because in the ACFT isomorphism (1.5) we have to take not the group F ×\\A × itself, but the group of its connected components;
L1147:  this may be taken into account by imposing
L1150: F
L1153: some restrictions on the one-dimensional representations of this group that we should consider.
L1155: 9A precise definition of automorphic representation is subtle because of the presence of continuous spectrum in the appropriate space of functions on
L1156: GLn(F)\\GLn(AF ); however, in what follows we will only consider those representations which are part of the discrete spectrum, so these difficulties will not
L1157: arise.
L1160: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 17
L1163: an elliptic curve E over Q, then we will obtain a two-dimensional Galois representation on its first ´etale cohomology. This representation contains a lot of
L1164: important information about the curve E, such as the number of points of E over Z/pZ for various primes p, as we will see below.
L1166: Recall that in the abelian case ACFT isomorphism (1.5) satisfied an important “rigidity” condition expressing the Frobenius element in the abelian Galois group
L1167: as a certain explicit ad\`ele (see formula (1.7)). The power of the Langlands correspondence is not just in the fact that we establish a correspondence between
L1168: objects of different nature, but that this correspondence again should satisfy a rigidity condition similar to the one in the abelian case. We will see below
L1169: that this rigidity condition implies that the intricate data on the Galois side, such as the number of points of E(Z/pZ), are translated into something more
L1170: tractable on the automorphic side, such as the coefficients in the q-expansion of the modular forms that encapsulate automorphic representations of GL2(AQ).
L1172: So, roughly speaking, one asks that under the Langlands correspondence certain natural invariants attached to the Galois representations and to the automorphic
L1173: representations be matched. These invariants are the Frobenius conjugacy classes on the Galois side and the Hecke eigenvalues on the automorphic side.
L1175: Let us explain this more precisely. We have already defined the Frobenius conjugacy classes. We just need to generalize this notion from finite extensions of F
L1176: to the infinite extension F. This is done as follows. For each prime ideal v in OF we choose a compatible system v of prime ideals that appear in the
L1177: factorization of v in all finite extensions of F. Such a system may be viewed as a prime ideal associated to v in the ring of integers of F. Then we attach to v
L1178:  its stabilizer in Gal(F/F), called the decomposition subgroup and denoted by Dv. We have a natural homomorphism (actually, an isomorphism) Dv → Gal(Fv,Fv).
L1179: Recall that Fv is the non-archimedian completion of F corresponding to v, and Fv is realized here as the completion of F at v. We denote by Ov the ring of
L1180: integers of Fv, by mv the unique maximal ideal of Ov, and by kv the (finite) residue field
L1183: ### OF /v = Ov/mv. The kernel of the composition
L1186: ### Dv → Gal(Fv,Fv) → Gal(kv/kv)
L1189: is called the inertia subgroup Iv of Dv. An_n-dimensional representation σ : Gal(F/F) → GLn is called unramified at v if Iv ⊂ Ker σ. Suppose that σ is
L1190: unramified at v. Let Frv be the geometric Frobenius automorphism in Gal(kv,kv) (the inverse to the operator x_7→ x \|kv\| acting on kv). In this case σ(Frv) is
L1191: a well-defined element of GLn. If we replace v by another compatible system of ideals, then σ(Frv) will get conjugated in GLn. So its conjugacy class is a
L1192: well-defined conjugacy class in GLn, which we call the Frobenius conjugacy class corresponding to v and σ.
L1194: This takes care of the Frobenius conjugacy classes. To explain what the Hecke eigenvalues are we need to look more closely at representations of the ad\`elic
L1195: group GLn(AF ), and
L1198: we will do that below. For now, let us just say that like the Frobenius conjugacy classes, the Hecke eigenvalues also correspond to conjugacy classes in GLn and
L1199:  are attached to all but finitely many prime ideals v of OF . As we will explain in the next section, in the case when_n = 2 they are related to the eigenvalues
L1200:  of the classical Hecke operators acting on modular forms.
L1203: ### 18  EDWARD FRENKEL
L1206: The matching condition alluded to above is then formulated as follows: if under the Langlands correspondence we have
L1209: σ −→ π, where σ is an_n-dimensional representation of Gal(F/F) and π is an automorphic representation of GLn(AF ), then the Frobenius conjugacy classes for σ
L1210: should coincide with the Hecke eigenvalues for π for almost all prime ideals v (precisely those v at which both σ and π are unramified). In the abelian case, n
L1211: = 1, this condition amounts precisely to the “rigidity” condition (1.7). In the next two sections we will see what this condition means in the non-abelian
L1212: case_n = 2 when σ comes from the first cohomology of an elliptic curve defined over Q. It turns out that in this special case the Langlands correspondence
L1213: becomes the statement of the Taniyama-Shimura conjecture which implies Fermat’s last theorem.
L1216: 1.6. Automorphic representations of GL2(AQ) and modular forms. In this subsection we discuss briefly cuspidal automorphic representations of GL2(A) = GL2(AQ)
L1217: and
L1220: how to relate them to classical modular forms on the upper half-plane. We will then consider the two-dimensional representations of Gal(Q/Q) arising from
L1221: elliptic curves defined
L1224: over Q and look at what the Langlands correspondence means for such representations.
L1226: We refer the reader to \[32, 33, 34, 35\] for more details on this subject.
L1228: Roughly speaking, cuspidal automorphic representations of GL2(A) are those irreducible representations of this group which occur in the discrete spectrum of a
L1229: certain space of functions on the quotient GL2(Q)\\GL2(A). Strictly speaking, this is not correct because the representations that we consider do not carry the
L1230: action of the factor GL2(R) of GL2(A), but only that of its Lie algebra gl2. Let us give a more precise definition.
L1232: We start by introducing the maximal compact subgroup K ⊂ GL2(A) which is equal to Q
L1235: p GL2(Zp) × O2. Let z be the center of the universal enveloping algebra of the

L1668: discussed before. Since the modular cusp form fπ encapsulates all the information about the automorphic representation π, we should be able to read them off the
L1669:  form fπ. It turns out that the operators H1,p have a simple interpretation in terms of functions on the upper half-plane.
L1671: Namely, they become the classical Hecke operators (see, e.g., \[32\] for an explicit formula).
L1673: Thus, we obtain that fπ is necessarily an eigenfunction of the classical Hecke operators.
L1675: Moreover, explicit calculation shows that if we normalize fπ as above, setting a1 = 1, then the eigenvalue h1,p will be equal to the pth coefficient ap in the
L1676: q-expansion of fπ. Let us summarize: to an irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation π (in the special case when χ ≡ 1 and ρ = k(k−2)/4, where_k ∈ Z>1) we
L1677:  have associated a modular cusp form fπ of weight_k and level N on the upper half-plane which is an eigenfunction of the classical Hecke operators
L1678: (corresponding to all primes that do not divide N) with the eigenvalues equal to the coefficients ap in the q-expansion of fπ.
L1681: 1.7. Elliptic curves and Galois representations. In the previous subsection we discussed some concrete examples of automorphic representations of GL2(A) that
L1682: can be
L1685: realized by classical modular cusp forms. Now we look at examples of the objects arising
L1688: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 23
L1691: on the other side of the Langlands correspondence, namely, two-dimensional representations of the Galois group of Q. Then we will see what matching their
L1692: invariants means.
L1695: As we mentioned above, one can construct representations of the Galois group of Q by taking the ´etale cohomology of algebraic varieties defined over Q. The
L1696: simplest example of a two-dimensional representation is thus provided by the first ´etale cohomology of an elliptic curve defined over Q, which (just as its
L1697: topological counterpart) is two-dimensional.
L1699: A smooth elliptic curve over Q may concretely be defined by an equation
L1702: 2 = x_3 + ax + b
L1705: y
L1708: ### 3 + 27b 2 6= 0. More precisely, this equation
L1711: where a,b are rational numbers such that 4a defines an affine curve E′
L1714: . The corresponding projective curve E is obtained by adding
L1717: to E′ a point at infinity; it is the curve in P 2 defined by the corresponding homogeneous equation.
L1719: The first ´etale cohomology H1 is isomorphic
L1722: ### ´et(EQ , Q\ell) of EQ with coefficients in Q\ell
L1725: to Q2
L1728: \ell . The definition of ´etale cohomology necessitates the choice of a prime \ell, but as
L1731: we will see below, important invariants of these representations, such as the Frobenius eigenvalues, are independent of \ell. This space may be concretely
L1732: realized as the dual of the Tate module of E, the inverse limit of the groups of points of order \ell n on E (with respect to the abelian group structure on E),
L1733:  tensored with Q\ell
L1736: . Since E is defined over
L1739: Q, the Galois group Gal(Q/Q) acts by symmetries on H1
L1742: ### ´et(EQ , Q\ell), and hence we obtain
L1745: a two-dimensional representation σE,\ell : Gal(Q/Q) → GL2(Q\ell). This representation is continuous with respect to the Krull topology14 on Gal(Q/Q) and the
L1746: usual \ell-adic topology on GL2(Q\ell).
L1748: What information can we infer from this representation? As explained in Sect. 1.5, important invariants of Galois representations are the eigenvalues of the
L1749: Frobenius conjugacy classes corresponding to the primes where the representation is unramified. In the
L1752: case at hand, the representation is unramified at the primes of “good reduction”, which do not divide an integer NE, the conductor of E. These Frobenius
L1753: eigenvalues have a nice interpretation. Namely, for p_6 \|NE we consider the sum of their inverses, which is the trace of σE(Frp). One can show that it is equal
L1754:  to Tr σE(Frp) = p + 1 − #E(Fp) where #E(Fp) is the number of points of E modulo p (see, \[33, 35\]). In particular, it is independent of \ell. Under the
L1755: Langlands correspondence, the representation σE of Gal(Q/Q) should correspond to a cuspidal automorphic representation π(σE) of the group GL2(A). Moreover, as
L1756: we discussed in Sect. 1.5, this correspondence should match the Frobenius eigenvalues of σE and the Hecke eigenvalues of π(σE). Concretely, in the case at hand,
L1757:  the matching condition is that Tr σE(Frp) should be equal to the eigenvalue h1,p of the Hecke operator H1,p, at all primes p where σE and π(σE) are unramified.
L1758:  It is not difficult to see that for this to hold, π(σE) must be a cuspidal automorphic representation of GL2(A) corresponding to a modular cusp form of
L1759: weight_k = 2. Therefore, if
L1762: 14in this topology the base of open neighborhoods of the identity is formed by normal subgroups of finite index (i.e., such that the quotient is a finite group)
L1765: ### 24  EDWARD FRENKEL
L1768: we believe in the Langlands correspondence, we arrive at the following startling conjecture: for each elliptic curve E over Q there should exist a modular cusp
L1769: form fE(q) = P∞ n
L1772: ### n=1 anq
L1775: \[
L1777: with a_1 = 1 and \tag{1.13}
L1779: \]
L1781: \[
L1783: ap = p + 1 - #E(Fp) for all but finitely many primes p! This is in fact the statement of the celebrated TaniyamaShimura conjecture that has recently been proved
L1784:  by A. Wiles and others [38]. It implies
L1786: \]
L1789: Fermat’s last theorem, see \[35\] and references therein.
L1791: In fact, the modular cusp form fE(q) is what is called a newform (this means that it does not come from a modular form whose level is a divisor of NE).
L1792: Moreover, the Galois representation σE and the automorphic representation π are unramified at exactly the same primes (namely, those which do not divide NE),
L1793: and formula (1.13) holds at all of those primes \[37\]. This way one obtains a bijection between isogeny classes of elliptic curves defined over Q with
L1794: conductor NE and newforms of weight_2 and level NE with integer Fourier coefficients.
L1796: One obtains similar statements by analyzing from the point of view of the Langlands correspondence the Galois representations coming from other algebraic
L1797: varieties, or more general motives.
L1800: 2. From number fields to function fields
L1803: As we have seen in the previous section, even special cases of the Langlands correspondence lead to unexpected number theoretic consequences. However, proving
L1804: these results
L1807: is notoriously difficult. Some of the difficulties are related to the special role played by the archimedian completion R in the ring of ad\`eles of Q (and
L1808: similarly, by the archimedian completions of other number fields). Representation theory of the archimedian factor GLn(R)
L1811: of the ad\`elic group GLn(AQ) is very different from that of the other, non-archimedian, factors GL2(Qp), and this leads to problems.
L1813: Fortunately, number fields have close cousins, called function fields, whose completions are all non-archimedian, so that the corresponding theory is more
L1814: uniform. The function field version of the Langlands correspondence turned out to be easier to handle than the correspondence in the number field case. In fact,
L1815:  it is now a theorem! First, V. Drinfeld \[39, 40\] proved it in the 80’s in the case of GL2, and more recently L. Lafforgue \[41\] proved it for GLn with an
L1816: arbitrary_n.
L1818: In this section we explain the analogy between number fields and function fields and formulate the Langlands correspondence for function fields.
L1821: 2.1. Function fields. What do we mean by a function field? Let X be a smooth projective connected curve over a finite field Fq. The field Fq(X) of (Fq-valued)
L1822: rational functions
L1825: on X is called the function field of X. For example, suppose that X = P
L1828: . Then Fq(X)
L1831: \[
L1833: is just the field of rational functions in one variable. Its elements are fractions P(t)/Q(t), where P(t) and Q(t) 6= 0 are polynomials over Fq without common
L1834: factors, with their usual operations of addition and multiplication. Explicitly, P(t) = PN_n n=0 pnt,pn \in Fq,
L1836: \]
L1839: and similarly for Q(t).
L1841: A general projective curve X over Fq is defined by a system of algebraic equations in the projective space P n over Fq. For example, we can define an elliptic
L1842: curve over Fq by
L1845: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 25
L1848: a cubic equation
L1851: ### (2.1)  y 2 3 + axz2 + bz3
L1854: z = x
L1857: ### , a,b,c ∈ Fq,
L1860: ### 2 15 What are the points of such a
L1863: written in homogeneous coordinates (x : y : z) of P
L1866: .
L1869: curve? Naively, these are the elements of the set X(Fq) of Fq-solutions of the equations defining this curve. For example, in the case of the elliptic curve
L1870: defined by the equation (2.1), this is the set of triples (x,y,z) ∈ F 3
L1873: satisfying (2.1), with two such triples identified
L1876: q
L1879: if they differ by an overall factor in F ×
L1882: q .
L1885: However, because the field Fq is not algebraically closed, we should also consider points
L1888: with values in the algebraic extensions Fqn of Fq. The situation is similar to a more familiar situation of a curve defined over the field of real numbers R.
L1889: For example, consider the
L1892: 2 + y_2 = −1. This equation has no solutions in R, curve over R defined by the equation x so naively we may think that this curve is empty. However, from the
L1893: algebraic point of view, we should think in terms of the ring of functions on this curve, which in this case is R = R[x,y]/(x_2 + y_2 + 1). Points of our curve
L1894: are maximal ideals of the ring R. The quotient R/I by such an ideal I is a field F called the residue field of this ideal. Thus, we have a surjective
L1895: homomorphism R → F whose kernel is I. The field F is necessarily a finite extension of R, so it could be either R or C. If it is R, then we may think of the
L1896: homomorphism R → F as sending a function f ∈ R on our curve to its value f(x) at some R-point x of our curve. That’s why maximal ideals of R with the residue
L1897: field R are the same as R-points of our curve. More generally, we will say that a maximal ideal I in R with the residue field F = R/I corresponds to an F-point
L1898: of our curve. In the case at hand it turns out that there are no R-points, but there are plenty of C-points, namely, all pairs
L1901: ### 2 2
L1904: of complex numbers (x0,y0) satisfying x
L1907: ### 0 + y 0 = −1. The corresponding homomorphism
L1910: \[
L1912: R \to C sends the generators x and y of R to x_0 and y_0 \in C.
L1914: \]
L1916: If we have a curve defined over Fq, then it has F-points, where F is a finite extension
L1919: of Fq, hence F ≃ Fqn,n > 0. An Fqn-point is defined as a maximal ideal of the ring of functions on an affine curve obtained by removing a point from our
L1920: projective curve,
L1923: with residue field Fqn. For example, in the case when the curve is P
L1926: , we can choose
L1929: the Fq-point ∞ as this point. Then we are left with the affine line A 1
L1932: , whose ring of
L1935: functions is the ring Fq\[t\] of polynomials in the variable t. The F-points of the affine line
L1938: are the maximal ideals of Fq[t] with residue field F. These are the same as the irreducible monic polynomials A(t) with coefficients in Fq. The corresponding
L1939: residue field is the field obtained by adjoining to Fq the roots of A(t). For instance, Fq-points correspond to the polynomials A(t) = t − a,a ∈ Fq. The set of
L1940: points of the projective line is therefore the
L1943: 1 together with the Fq-point ∞ that has been removed.16
L1946: set of all points of A
L1949: 15Elliptic curves over finite fields Fp have already made an appearance in the previous section. However, their role there was different: we had started with an
L1950:  elliptic curve E defined over Z and used it to define a representation of the Galois group Gal(Q/Q) in the first ´etale cohomology of E. We then related the
L1951: trace of the Frobenius element Frp for a prime p on this representation to the number of Fp-points of the elliptic curve over Fp obtained by reduction of E mod
L1952: p. In contrast, in this section we use an elliptic curve, or a more general smooth projective curve X, over a field Fq that is fixed once and for all. This
L1953: curve defines a function field Fq(X) that, as we argue in this section, should be viewed as analogous to the field Q of rational numbers, or a more general
L1954: number field.

L1957:  union of the sets of points of those affine curves (each

L2075: Before we get to that, we want to comment on why is it that we only consider curves and not higher dimensional varieties. The point is that while function
L2076: fields of curves are very similar to number fields, the fields of functions on higher dimensional varieties have a very different structure. For example, if X
L2077: is a smooth surface, then the completions of the field of rational functions on X are labeled by pairs: a point x of X and a germ of a curve passing through x.
L2078: The corresponding complete field is isomorphic to the field of formal power series in two variables. At the moment no one knows how to formulate an analogue of
L2079: the Langlands correspondence for the field of functions on an algebraic variety of dimension greater than one, and finding such a formulation is a very
L2080: important open problem. There is an analogue of the abelian class field theory (see \[42\]), but not much is known beyond that. In Part III of this paper we
L2081: will argue that the Langlands correspondence for the function fields of curves – transported to the realm of complex curves – is closely related to the
L2082: two-dimensional conformal field theory. The hope is, of course, that there is a similar connection between a higher dimensional Langlands correspondence and
L2083: quantum field theories in dimensions greater than two (see, e.g., \[43\] for a discussion of this analogy). 2.2. Galois representations. Let X be a smooth
L2084: connected projective curve over_k = Fq and F = k(X) the field of rational functions on X. Consider the Galois group Gal(F/F). It is instructive to think of the
L2085: Galois group of a function field as a kind of fundamental group of X. Indeed, if Y → X is a covering of X, then the field_k(Y ) of rational functions on Y is an
L2086:  extension of the field F = k(X) of rational functions on X, and the Galois group Gal(k(Y )/k(X)) may be viewed as the group of “deck transformations” of the
L2087: cover. If our cover is unramified, then this group may be identified with a quotient of the fundamental group of X. Otherwise, this group is isomorphic to a
L2088: quotient of the fundamental group of X without the ramification points. The Galois group Gal(F/F) itself may be viewed as the group of “deck transformations” of
L2089:  the maximal (ramified) cover of X.

L2539: What is remarkable about Theorem 1 is that it is such a “clean” statement: there is a bijection between the isomorphism classes of appropriately defined Galois
L2540: representations and automorphic representations. Such a bijection is impossible in the number field case: we do not expect that all automorphic representations

L2550: The situation in the function field case is so much nicer partly because the function field is defined geometrically (via algebraic curves), and this allows the
L2551:  usage of techniques and methods that are not yet available for number fields (surely, it also helps that F does not have any archimedian completions). It is
L2552: natural to ask whether the Langlands correspondence could be formulated purely geometrically, for algebraic curves over an arbitrary field, not necessarily a
L2553: finite field. We will discuss this in the next part of this survey.

L2560: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 33
L2563: ### Part II. The geometric Langlands Program
L2566: The geometric reformulation of the Langlands conjecture allows one to state it for curves defined over an arbitrary field, not just over finite fields. For
L2567: instance, it may be stated for complex curves, and in this setting one can apply methods of complex algebraic geometry which are unavailable over finite fields.
L2568:  Hopefully, this formulation will eventually help us understand better the general underlying patterns of the Langlands correspondence. In this section we will
L2569: formulate the geometric Langlands conjecture for GLn. In particular, we will explain how moduli spaces of rank n vector bundles on algebraic curves naturally
L2570: come into play. We will then show how to use the geometry of the simplest of these moduli spaces, the Picard variety, to prove the geometric Langlands
L2571: correspondence for GL1, following P. Deligne. Next, we will generalize the geometric Langlands correspondence to the case of an arbitrary reductive group. We
L2572: will also discuss the connection between this correspondence over the field of complex numbers and the Fourier-Mukai transform.
L2575: 3. The geometric Langlands conjecture
L2578: What needs to be done to reformulate the Langlands conjecture geometrically? We have to express the two key notions used in the classical set-up: the Galois
L2579: representations and the automorphic representations, geometrically, so that they make sense for a curve defined over, say, the field of complex numbers.
L2581: 3.1. Galois representations as local systems. Let X be again a curve over a finite field_k, and F = k(X) the field of rational functions on X. As we indicated
L2582: in Sect. 2.2, the Galois group Gal(F/F) should be viewed as a kind of fundamental group, and so its representations unramified away from a finite set of points
L2583: S should be viewed as local systems on X\\S. The notion of a local system makes sense if X is defined over other fields. The main case of interest to us is when
L2584:  X is a smooth projective curve over C, or equivalently, a compact Riemann surface. Then by a local system on X we understand a locally constant sheaf F of
L2585: vector spaces on X, in the analytic topology of X in which the base of open neighborhoods of a point x ∈ X is formed by small discs centered at x (defined with
L2586: respect to a particular metric in the conformal class of X). This should be contrasted with the Zariski topology of X in which open neighborhoods of x ∈ X are
L2587: complements of finitely many points of X. More concretely, for each open analytic subset U of X we have a C-vector space F(U) of sections of F over U satisfying
L2588:  the usual compatibilities22 and for each point x ∈ X there is an open neighborhood Ux such that the restriction of F to Ux is isomorphic to the 22namely, we

L2619: way to realize local systems which will be especially convenient for us: by defining a complex vector bundle on X equipped with a flat connection. A complex
L2620: vector bundle E by itself does not give us a local system, because while E can be trivialized on sufficiently small open analytic subsets Uα ⊂ X, the transition
L2621:  functions on the overlaps Uα ∩ Uβ will in general be non-constant functions Uα ∩ Uβ → GLn(C). To make them constant, we need an additional rigidity on E which
L2622: would give us a preferred system of trivializations on each open subset such that on the overlaps they would differ only by constant transition functions. Such
L2623: a system is provided by the data of a flat connection. Recall that a flat connection on E is a system of operations \nabla, defined for each open subset U ⊂ X
L2624: and compatible on overlaps, \nabla : Vect(U) → End(Γ(U, E)), which assign to a vector field ξ on U a linear operator \nabla ξ on the space Γ(U, E) of smooth
L2625: sections of E on U. It must satisfy the Leibniz rule (3.1) \nabla ξ(fs) = f\nabla ξ(s) + (ξ · f)s, f ∈ C ∞(U),s ∈ Γ(U, E), and also the conditions (3.2) \nabla
L2626: fξ = f\nabla ξ, [\nabla ξ, \nabla η] = \nabla[ξ,η] (the last condition is the flatness). Given a flat connection, the local horizontal sections (i.e., those
L2627: annihilated by all \nabla ξ) provide us with the preferred systems of local trivializations (or equivalently, identifications of nearby fibers) that we were
L2628: looking for. Note that if X is a complex manifold, like it is in our case, then the connection has two parts: holomorphic and anti-holomorphic, which are
L2629: defined with respect to the complex structure on X. The anti-holomorphic (or (0, 1)) part of the connection consists of the operators \nabla ξ, where ξ runs
L2630: over the anti-holomorphic vector fields on U ⊂ X. It gives us
L2633: ### 23for which the space F(U) is a fixed vector space C
L2636: n and all restriction maps are isomorphisms
L2639: \[
L2641: 24these elements must satisfy the cocycle condition g\alpha \gamma = g\alpha \beta g\beta \gamma on each triple intersection U\alpha ∩U\beta ∩U\gamma
L2643: \]
L2646: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 35
L2649: a holomorphic structure on E: namely, we declare the holomorphic sections to be those which are annihilated by the anti-holomorphic part of the connection.
L2650: Thus, a complex bundle E equipped with a flat connection \nabla automatically becomes a holomorphic bundle on X. Conversely, if E is already a holomorphic
L2651: vector bundle on a complex manifold X, then to define a connection on E that is compatible with the holomorphic structure on E all we need to do is to define is
L2652:  a holomorphic flat connection. By definition, this is just a collection of operators \nabla ξ, where ξ runs over all holomorphic vector fields on U ⊂ X,
L2653: satisfying conditions (3.1) and (3.2), where f is now a holomorphic function on U and s is a holomorphic section of E over U.
L2655: In particular, if X is a complex curve, then locally, with respect to a local holomorphic coordinate z on X and a local trivialization of E, all we need to
L2656: define is an operator
L2659: ### ∂
L2662: \[
L2664: \nabla \partial /\partial z = \partial z +A(z), where A(z) is a matrix valued holomorphic function. These operators
L2666: \]
L2669: must satisfy the usual compatibility conditions on the overlaps. Because there is only one such operator on each open set, the resulting connection is
L2670: automatically flat.
L2672: Given a vector bundle E with a flat connection \nabla on X (or equivalently, a holomorphic vector bundle on X with a holomorphic connection), we obtain a
L2673: locally constant sheaf (i.e., a local system) on X as the sheaf of horizontal sections of E with respect to \nabla.
L2675: This construction in fact sets up an equivalence of the two categories if X is compact (for example, a smooth projective curve). This is called the
L2676: Riemann-Hilbert correspondence.
L2678: More generally, in the Langlands correspondence we consider local systems defined on the non-compact curves X\\S, where X is a projective curve and S is a
L2679: finite set. Such local systems are called ramified at the points of S. In this case the above equivalence of categories is valid only if we restrict ourselves
L2680: to holomorphic bundles with holomorphic connections with regular singularities at the points of the set S (that means that the order of pole of the connection
L2681: at a point in S is at most_1). However, in this paper (with the exception of Sect. 9.8) we will restrict ourselves to unramified local systems. In general, we
L2682: expect that vector bundles on curves with connections that have singularities, regular or irregular, also play an important role in the geometric Langlands
L2683: correspondence, see \[44\]; we discuss this in Sect. 9.8 below.
L2685: To summarize, we believe that we have found the right substitute for the (unramified) n-dimensional Galois representations in the case of a compact complex
L2686: curve X: these are the rank n local systems on X, or equivalently, rank n holomorphic vector bundles on X with a holomorphic connection.
L2689: 3.2. Ad\`eles and vector bundles. Next, we wish to interpret geometrically the objects appearing on the other side of the Langlands correspondence, namely, the
L2690: automorphic representations. This will turn out to be more tricky. The essential point here is the interpretation of automorphic representations in terms of the
L2691:  moduli spaces of rank n vector bundles.

L2750: We use the following observation: any rank n vector bundle V on X can be trivialized over the complement of finitely many points. This is equivalent to the
L2751: existence of n meromorphic sections of V whose values are linearly independent away from finitely many points of X. These sections can be constructed as
L2752: follows: choose a non-zero meromorphic section of V. Then, over the complement of its zeros and poles, this section spans a line

L2759: section of V in an arbitrary way, we obtain two sections of V which are linearly independent away from finitely many points of X. Continuing like this, we
L2760: construct n meromorphic sections of V satisfying the above conditions.

L2864: 3.3. From functions to sheaves. Thus, when X is a curve over Fq, irreducible unramified automorphic representations π are encoded by the automorphic functions
L2865: fπ, which are functions on GLn(F)\\GLn(A)/GLn(O). This double quotient makes perfect sense when X is defined over C and is in fact the set of isomorphism
L2866: classes of rank_n bundles on X. But what should replace the notion of an automorphic function fπ in this case?
L2868: We will argue that the proper analogue is not a function, as one might naively expect, but a sheaf on the corresponding algebro-geometric object: the moduli
L2869: stack Bunn of rank n bundles on X.
L2871: This certainly requires a leap of faith. The key step is the Grothendieck fonctionsfaisceaux dictionary. Let V be an algebraic variety over Fq. Then,
L2872: according to Grothendieck
L2875: , the “correct” geometric counterpart of the notion of a (Q\ell -valued) function on the
L2878: set of Fq-points of V is the notion of a complex of \ell-adic sheaves on V . A precise definition of an \ell-adic sheaf would take us too far afield. Let us
L2879: just say that the simplest example of an \ell-adic sheaf is an \ell-adic local system, which is, roughly speaking, a locally constant Q\ell - sheaf on V (in the
L2880:  ´etale topology).27 For a general \ell-adic sheaf there exists a stratification of V by locally closed subvarieties Vi such that the sheaves F\|Vi are locally
L2881: constant.
L2884: 26the passage to the formal discs is justified by an analogue of the “strong approximation theorem” that was mentioned in Sect. 1.6 27The precise definition
L2885: (see, e.g., \[45, 46\]) is more subtle: a typical example is a compatible system of locally constant Z/\ell nZ-sheaves for_n > 0

L2892: this morphism will

L2905: F at x, a

L2941: The map K → fK intertwines the natural operations on complexes of sheaves with natural operations on functions (see \[47\], Sect. 1.2). For example, pull-back
L2942: of a sheaf corresponds to the pull-back of a function, and push-forward of a sheaf with compact support corresponds to the fiberwise integration of a
L2943: function.28
L2946: Thus, because of the existence of the Frobenius automorphism in the Galois group Gal(Fq/Fq) (which is the group of symmetries of an Fq-point) we can pass from
L2947: \ell-adic sheaves to functions on any algebraic variety over Fq. This suggests that the proper geometrization of the notion of a function in this setting is the
L2948:  notion of \ell-adic sheaf.
L2950: The passage from complexes of sheaves to functions is given by the alternating sum of cohomologies. Hence what matters is not K itself, but the corresponding
L2951: object of the derived category of sheaves. However, the derived category is too big, and there are many objects of the derived category which are non-zero, but
L2952: whose function is equal to zero.
L2954: For example, consider a complex of the form 0 → F → F → 0 with the zero differential.
L2956: It has non-zero cohomologies in degrees_0 and_1, and hence is a non-zero object of the derived category. But the function associated to it is identically zero.
L2957: That is why it would be useful to identify a natural abelian category C in the derived category of \ell-adic sheaves such that the map assigning to an object K
L2958: ∈ C the function fK gives rise to an injective map from the Grothendieck group of C to the space of functions on V . 29 The naive category of \ell-adic sheaves
L2959: (included into the derived category as the subcategory whose objects are the complexes situated in cohomological degree_0) is not a good
L2962: choice for various reasons; for instance, it is not stable under the Verdier duality. The correct choice turns out to be the abelian category of perverse
L2963: sheaves.
L2965: What is a perverse sheaf? It is not really a sheaf, but a complex of \ell-adic sheaves on V satisfying certain restrictions on the degrees of their non-zero
L2966: stalk cohomologies

L2984: (see [48, 49, 50, 51]).30 Examples are \ell-adic local systems on a smooth variety V , placed in cohomological degree equal to − dim V . General perverse
L2985: sheaves are “glued” from such local systems defined on the strata of a particular stratification V = S Vi of V by
L2988: i
L2991: locally closed subvarieties. Even though perverse sheaves are complexes of sheaves, they form an abelian subcategory inside the derived category of sheaves, so
L2992: we can work with them like with ordinary sheaves. Unlike the ordinary sheaves though, the perverse sheaves have the following remarkable property: an
L2993: irreducible perverse sheaf on a variety V is completely determined by its restriction to an arbitrary open dense subset (provided that this restriction is
L2994: non-zero). For more on this, see Sect. 5.4.Experience shows that many “interesting” functions on the set V (Fq) of points of an algebraic variety V over Fq are
L2995: of the form fK for a perverse sheaf K on V . Unramified automorphic functions on GLn(F)\\GLn(A)/GLn(O) certainly qualify as “interesting”
L2998: functions. Can we obtain them from perverse sheaves on some algebraic variety underlying the set GLn(F)\\GLn(A)/GLn(O)?
L3000: In order to do that we need to interpret the set GLn(F)\\GLn(A)/GLn(O) as the set of Fq-points of an algebraic variety over Fq. Lemma 2 gives us a hint as to
L3001: what this variety should be: the moduli space of rank n vector bundles on the curve X.
L3003: Unfortunately, for_n > 1 there is no algebraic variety whose set of Fq-points is the set of isomorphism classes of all rank_n bundles on X. 31 The reason is
L3004: that bundles have groups of automorphisms, which vary from bundle to bundle. So in order to define the structure of an algebraic variety we need to throw away
L3005: the so-called unstable bundles, whose groups of automorphisms are too large, and glue together the so-called semi-stable bundles. Only the points corresponding
L3006: to the so-called stable bundles will survive. But an automorphic function is a priori defined on the set of isomorphism classes of all bundles. Therefore we do
L3007: not want to throw away any of them.32 The solution is to consider the moduli stack Bunn of rank_n bundles on X. It is not an algebraic variety, but it looks
L3008: locally like the quotient of an algebraic variety by the action of an algebraic group (these actions are not free, and therefore the quotient is no longer an
L3009: algebraic variety). For a nice introduction to algebraic stacks, see \[52\]. Examples of stacks familiar to physicists include the Deligne-Mumford stack of
L3010: stable curves of a fixed genus and the moduli stacks of stable maps. In these cases the groups of automorphisms are actually finite, so these stacks may be
L3011: viewed as orbifolds. The situation is more complicated for vector bundles, for which the groups of automorphisms are typically continuous. The corresponding
L3012: moduli stacks are called Artin stacks. For example, even in
L3015: the case of line bundles, each of them has a continuous groups of automorphisms, namely, the multiplicative group. What saves the day is the fact that the group
L3016:  of automorphisms is the same for all line bundles. This is not true for bundles of rank higher than 1.The technique developed in \[53, 15\] allows us to define
L3017:  sheaves on algebraic stacks and to operate with these sheaves in ways that we are accustomed to when working with algebraic varieties. So the moduli stack Bunn
L3018:  will be sufficient for our purposes.
L3021: \[
L3023: 30more precisely, a perverse sheaf is an object of the derived category of sheaves 31for_n = 1, the Picard variety of X may be viewed as the moduli space of
L3024: line bundles 32actually, one can show that each cuspidal automorphic function vanishes on a subset of unstable bundles (see [55],Lemma_6.11), and this opens up
L3025: the possibility that somehow moduli spaces of semistable bundles would suffice
L3027: \]

L3033: Thus, we have now identified the geometric objects which should replace unramified automorphic functions: these should be perverse sheaves on the moduli stack
L3034: Bunn of rank_n bundles on our curve X. The concept of perverse sheaf makes perfect sense for varieties over C (see, e.g., \[49, 50, 51\]), and this allows us to
L3035:  formulate the geometric Langlands conjecture when X (and hence Bunn) is defined over C. But over the field of complex numbers there is one more reformulation
L3036: that we can make, namely, we can to pass from perverse sheaves to D-modules. We now briefly discuss this last reformulation.
L3039: 3.4. From perverse sheaves to D-modules. If V is a smooth complex algebraic variety, we can define the sheaf DV of algebraic differential operators on V (in

L3059: module F(U) over D(U), and these modules satisfy the usual compatibilities.
L3061: The simplest example of a DV -module is the sheaf of holomorphic sections of a holomorphic vector bundle E on V equipped with a holomorphic (more precisely,

L3066: the former on E(U) in the usual way, and the latter by means of the holomorphic connection. In the special case when E is the trivial bundle with the trivial
L3067: connection, its sheaf of sections is the sheaf OV of holomorphic functions on V .

L3070: commutative and is in fact isomorphic to the free polynomial algebra DV = C[D1,... ,DN ], where D1,... ,DN are some global differential operators on V . We will

L3115: As we discussed above, the sheaf of horizontal sections of a holomorphic vector bundle E with a holomorphic flat connection on V is a locally constant sheaf (in
L3116:  the analytic, not Zariski, topology!), which becomes a perverse sheaf after the shift in cohomological degree by dimC V . The corresponding functor from the
L3117: category of bundles with flat connection on V to the category of locally constant sheaves on V may be extended to a functor from the category of holonomic
L3118: D-modules to the category of perverse sheaves.
L3120: A priori this functor sends a D-module to an object of the derived category of sheaves, but one shows that it is actually an object of the abelian subcategory
L3121: of perverse sheaves.
L3123: This provides another explanation why the category of perverse sheaves is the “right” abelian subcategory of the derived category of sheaves (as opposed to the
L3124: naive abelian subcategory of complexes concentrated in cohomological degree 0, for example). This functor is called the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence. For
L3125: instance, this functor assigns to a holonomic D-module (3.4) on V the sheaf whose sections over an open analytic subset U ⊂ V is the space of holomorphic
L3126: functions on T that are solutions of the system (3.5)on U. In the next section we will see how this works in a simple example.

L3268: | 3.6. More on D-modules. One of the lessons that we should learn from this elementary |  |
L3270: | example is that when our differential equations (3.5) have regular singularities, as is the |  |

L3285: |  | The Riemann-Hilbert functor Sol sets up an equivalence between the category of holonomic D-modules with regular singularities on V (such as the D-module
L3286: that we considered |
L3289: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 43
L3292: above) and the category of perverse sheaves on V . This equivalence is called the RiemannHilbert correspondence (see \[49, 50, 51, 54\]).34 Therefore we may
L3293: replace perverse sheaves
L3296: on smooth algebraic varieties (or algebraic stacks, see \[15\]) over C by holonomic D-modules with regular singularities.
L3298: Under this equivalence of categories natural operations (functors) on perverse sheaves, such as the standard operations of direct and inverse images, go to
L3299: certain operations on D-modules. We will not describe these operations here in detail referring the reader to [49, 50, 51, 54]). But one way to think about them
L3300:  which is consistent with the point of view presented above as as follows. If we think of a D-module F as something that encodes a system of differential
L3301: equations, then applying an operation to F, such as the inverse or direct image, corresponds to applying the same type of operation (pull-back in the case of
L3302: inverse image, an integral in the case of direct image) to the solutions of the system of differential equations encoded by F. So the solutions of the system of
L3303:  differential equations encoded by the inverse or direct image of F are the pull-backs or the integrals of the solutions of the system encoded by F,
L3304: respectively.
L3306: The fact that natural operations on D-modules correspond to natural operations on their solutions (which are functions) provides another point of view on the
L3307: issue why, when moving from a finite field to C, we decided to replace the notion of a function by the notion of a D-module. We may think that there is actually
L3308:  a function, or perhaps a vector space of functions, lurking in the background, but these functions may be too complicated to write down - they may be
L3309: multi-valued and have nasty singularities (for more on this, see Sect. 9.5). For all intents and purposes it might be better to write down the system of
L3310: differential equations that these functions satisfy, i.e, consider the corresponding D-module, instead.
L3312: Let us summarize: we have seen that an automorphic representation may be encapsulated by an automorphic function on the set of isomorphism classes of rank n
L3313: vector
L3316: bundles on the curve X. We then apply the following progression to the notion of “function”
L3319: \[
L3321: functions over Fq =\Rightarrow \ell -adic sheaves =\Rightarrow perverse sheaves over C =\Rightarrow D-modules
L3323: \]
L3325: 
L3326: and end up with the notion of “D-module” instead. This leads us to believe that the proper replacement for the notion of automorphic representation in the case
L3327: of a curve X over C is the notion of D-module on the moduli stack Bunn of rank n vector bundles on X. In order to formulate precisely the geometric Langlands
L3328: correspondence we need to figure out what properties these D-modules should satisfy.

L3671: defined pointwise while the latter are defined globally on the curve X. In the classical setting therefore it was not clear whether for a given automorphic
L3672: representation π one could always find a Galois representation (or an \ell-adic local system) with the same Frobenius eigenvalues as the Hecke eigenvalues of π
L3673: (part of Theorem_1 is the statement that there is always a unique one). In the geometric setting this question is mute, because the very notion of a Hecke
L3674: eigensheaf presumes that we know what its eigenvalue E is. That is why the geometric Langlands correspondence in the geometric setting is a map in one
L3675: direction: from local systems to Hecke eigensheaves.
