L3704: This theorem was proved by Deligne for GL1 (we recall it in the next section) and by Drinfeld in the case of GL2 \[39\] (see \[28\], Sect. 6, for a review).
L3705: These works motivated the conjecture in the case of GLn, which has been proved in \[55, 56\] (these works were
L3708: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 47
L3711: also influenced by \[57, 58\]). In the case when X is over C we can replace “perverse sheaf” in the statement of Theorem_3 by “D-module”.36 The reader may be
L3712: wondering what has become of the cuspidality condition, which was imposed in Sect. 2.3. It has a transparent geometric analogue (see \[57, 55\]). As shown in
L3713: \[55\], the geometric cuspidality condition is automatically satisfied for the Hecke eigensheaves AutE associated in \[55\] to irreducible local systems E. One
L3714: cannot emphasize enough the importance of the fact that E is an irreducible rank_n local system on X in the statement Theorem_3. It is only in this case that we
L3715:  expect the Hecke eigensheaf AutE to be as nice as described in the theorem. Moreover, in this case we expect that AutE is unique up to an isomorphism. If E is
L3716: not irreducible, then the situation becomes more complicated. For example, Hecke eigensheaves corresponding to local systems that are direct sums of_n rank_1
L3717: local systems – the so-called geometric Eisenstein series – have been constructed in \[59, 60, 61\]. The best case scenario is
L3720: when these rank 1 local systems are pairwise non-isomorphic. The corresponding Hecke eigensheaf is a direct sum of infinitely many irreducible perverse sheaves
L3721: on Bunn, labeled by the lattice Z n
L3724: . More general geometric Eisenstein series are complexes of perverse
L3727: sheaves. Moreover, it is expected that in general there are several non-isomorphic Hecke eigensheaves corresponding to such a local system, so it is appropriate
L3728:  to talk not about a single Hecke eigensheaf AutE, but a category AutE of Hecke eigensheaves with eigenvalue E.
L3730: An object of AutE is by definition a collection (K,ıi), where K is a Hecke eigensheaf with eigenvalue E and ıi are isomorphisms (3.9). In general, we should
L3731: allow objects to be complexes (not necessarily perverse sheaves), but in principle there are several candidates for AutE depending on what kinds of complexes we
L3732:  allow (bounded, unbounded, etc.).
L3734: The group of automorphisms of E naturally acts on the category AutE. Namely, to
L3737: ) to
L3740: \[
L3742: an automorphism g of E we assign the functor AutE \to AutE sending (F,{ıi}\lambda \in P+ (F,{g ◦ıi}\lambda \in P+). For example,in the case when E is the direct
L3743:  sum of rank_1 local systems that are pairwise non-isomorphic,the group of automorphisms of E is the_n-dimensional torus. Its action on the geometric Eisenstein
L3744:  series sheaf constructed in [59,61] amounts to a Z_n
L3746: \]
L3749: -grading on this sheaf, which comes from the construction expressing it as a direct
L3752: sum of irreducible objects labeled by Z n
L3755: . For non-abelian groups of automorphisms the
L3758: corresponding action will be more sophisticated.
L3760: This means that, contrary to our naive expectations, the most difficult rank n local system on X is the trivial local system E0. Its group of automorphisms is
L3761: GLn which acts non-trivially on the corresponding category AutE0
L3764: . Some interesting Hecke eigensheaves
L3767: are unbounded complexes in this case, and a precise definition of the corresponding category that would include such complexes is an open problem \[62\]. Note

L3828: is a “complex of trivial local systems” on X. Remembering the cohomological degree shift in formula (3.9), we see that formula (3.10) may be interpreted as
L3829: saying that the constant sheaf on Bunn is a Hecke eigensheaf with eigenvalue E′
L3832: 0 .
L3835: The Hecke eigenfunction corresponding to the constant sheaf is the just the constant function on GLn(F)\\GLn(A)/GLn(O), which corresponds to the trivial
L3836: one-dimensional representation of the ad\`elic group GLn(A). The fact that the “eigenvalue” E′ is not a
L3839: local system, but a complex, indicates that something funny is going on with the trivial representation. In fact, it has to do with the so-called “Arthur’s SL2”
L3840:  part of the parameter of a general automorphic representation \[63\]. The precise meaning of this is beyond the scope of the present article, but the idea is
L3841: as follows. Arthur has conjectured that if we want to consider unitary automorphic representations of GLn(A) that are not necessarily cuspidal, then the true
L3842: parameters for those are_n-dimensional representations not of Gal(F/F), but of the product Gal(F/F) × SL2. The homomorphisms whose restriction to the SL2 factor
L3843:  are trivial correspond to the so-called tempered representations. In the case of GLn all cuspidal unitary representations are tempered, so the SL2 factor does
L3844: not play a role. But what about the trivial representation of GLn(A)? It is unitary, but certainly not tempered (nor cuspidal). According to \[63\], the
L3845: corresponding parameter is the the_n-dimensional representation of Gal(F/F) × SL2, which is trivial on the first factor and is the irreducible representation of
L3846:  the second factor. One can argue that it is this non-triviality of the action of Arthur’s SL2 that is observed geometrically in the cohomological grading
L3847: discussed above.
L3849: In any case, this is a useful example to consider.
L3852: 4. Geometric abelian class field theory
L3855: In this section we discuss the geometric Langlands correspondence for_n = 1, i.e., for rank one local systems. This is a particularly simple case, which is well
L3856:  understood. Still,
L3859: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 49
L3862: it already contains the germs of some of the ideas and constructions that we will use for local systems of higher rank.
L3864: Note that because CP1 is simply-connected, there is only one (unramified) rank one local system on it, so the (unramified) geometric Langlands correspondence is
L3865:  vacuous in this case. Hence throughout this section we will assume that the genus of X is positive.

L4178: 4.3. Another take for curves over C. In the case when X is a complex curve, there is a more direct construction of the local system Aut0

L4186: realize the Jacobian as the quotient H1 (X, OX )/H1 (X,Z) ≃ C g/H1 (X,Z). Thus, we obtain a homomorphism π1(Jac) → C ×, which gives us a rank one local system
L4187: EJac on Jac. We claim that this is Aut0 E. We can then construct Autd

L4258: 4.4. Connection to the Fourier-Mukai transform. As we saw at the end of the previous section, the construction of the Hecke eigensheaf AutE associated to a rank
L4259:  one local system E on a complex curve X (the case_n = 1) is almost tautological: we use the fact that the fundamental group of Jac is the maximal abelian
L4260: quotient of the fundamental group of X. However, one can strengthen the statement of the geometric Langlands conjecture by interpreting it in the framework of
L4261: the Fourier-Mukai transform. Let Loc1 be the moduli space of rank one local systems on X. A local system is a pair (F, \nabla), where F is a holomorphic line
L4262: bundle and \nabla is a holomorphic connection on F. Since F supports a holomorphic (hence flat) connection, the first Chern class of F, which is the degree of
L4263: F, has to vanish. Therefore F defines a point of Pic0 = Jac. Thus, we obtain a natural map p : Loc1 → Jac sending (F, \nabla) to F. What are the fibers of this
L4264: map? The fiber of p over F is the space of holomorphic connections on F. Given a connection \nabla on F, any other connection can be written uniquely as \nabla′
L4265:  = \nabla+ω, where ω is a holomorphic one-form on X. It is clear that any F supports a holomorphic connection. Therefore the fiber of p over F is an affine
L4266: space over the vector space H0 (X, Ω) of holomorphic oneforms on X. Thus, Loc1 is an affine bundle over Jac over the trivial vector bundle with the fiber H0 (X,
L4267:  Ω). This vector bundle is naturally identified with the cotangent bundle T ∗ Jac. Indeed, the tangent space to Jac at a point corresponding to a line bundle F
L4268: is the (X,End F) = H1 (X, OX ). Therefore space of infinitesimal deformations of F, which is H1 its dual is isomorphic to H0 (X, Ω) by the Serre duality.
L4269: Therefore Loc1 is what is called the twisted cotangent bundle to Jac. As we explained in the previous section, a holomorphic line bundle with a holomorphic
L4270: connection on X is the same thing as a holomorphic line bundle with a flat holomorphic connection on Jac, E = (F, \nabla) 7→ EJac = Aut0 E. Therefore Loc1 may
L4271: be interpreted as the moduli space of pairs (Fe, \nabla e), where Fe is a holomorphic line bundle on Jac and \nabla e is a flat holomorphic connection on Fe.
L4272: Now consider the product Loc1 × Jac. Over it we have the “universal flat holomorphic line bundle” P, whose restriction to (Fe, \nabla e )× Jac is the line
L4273: bundle with connection (Fe, \nabla e) on Jac. It has a partial flat connection along Jac, i.e., we can differentiate its sections
L4276: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 53
L4279: along Jac using \nabla e. Thus, we have the following diagram:
L4282: P
L4285: ### ↓

L4316: considered as a D-module on Jac. So the simplest O-modules on Loc1, namely, the skyscraper sheaves supported at points, go to the simplest D-modules on Jac,
L4317: namely, flat line bundles, which are the (degree zero components of) the Hecke eigensheaves AutE. We should compare this picture to the picture of Fourier
L4318: transform. The Fourier transform sends the delta-functions δx,x ∈ R (these are the analogues of the skyscraper sheaves) to the exponential functions e ixy,y ∈
L4319: R, which can be viewed as the simplest D-modules ixy is the solution of the differential equation (∂y − ix)Φ(y) = 0, so it corresponds to the trivial line
L4320: bundle on R with the connection \nabla = ∂y − ix. Now, it is quite

L4326: clear that a general function in x can be thought of as an integral, or superposition, of the delta-functions δx,x ∈ R. The main theorem of the Fourier analysis
L4327:  is that the Fourier transform is an isomorphism (of the appropriate function spaces). It may be viewed, loosely, as the statement that on the other side of the
L4328:  transform the exponential functions ixy,x ∈ R, also form a good “basis” for functions. In other words, other functions can be e written as Fourier integrals.
L4329: An analogous thing happens in our situation. It has been shown by G. Laumon \[65\] and M. Rothstein \[66\] that the functors F and G give rise to mutually

L4375: ### 54  EDWARD FRENKEL
L4378: This is actually consistent with the picture emerging from the classical Langlands correspondence. In the classical Langlands correspondence (when X is a curve
L4379: over Fq) the Hecke eigenfunctions on GLn(F)\\GLn(A)/GLn(O) form a basis of the appropriate space of functions on GLn(F)\\GLn(A)/GLn(O).39 That is why we should
L4380: expect that the geometric objects that replace the Hecke eigenfunctions – namely, the Hecke eigensheaves on Bunn – should give us a kind of “spectral
L4381: decomposition” of the derived category of Dmodules on Bun0
L4384: n . The Laumon-Rothstein theorem may be viewed a precise formulation
L4387: of this statement.
L4389: The above equivalence is very closely related to the Fourier-Mukai transform. Let us recall that the Fourier-Mukai transform is an equivalence between the
L4390: derived categories of coherent sheaves on an abelian variety A and its dual A∨, which is the moduli space of line bundles on A (and conversely, A is the moduli
L4391: space of line bundles on A∨). Then we have the universal (also known as the Poincar´e) line bundle P on A∨ ×A whose restriction to a ∨ × a, where a ∨ ∈ A∨, is
L4392: the line bundle L(a ∨) corresponding to a ∨ (and likewise for the restriction to A∨ × a). Then we have functors between the derived categories of coherent
L4393: sheaves (of O-modules) on A and A∨ defined in the same way as in formula (4.5), which set up an equivalence of categories, called the Fourier-Mukai transform.
L4395: Rothstein and Laumon have generalized the Fourier-Mukai transform by replacing A∨, which is the moduli space of line bundles on A, by A♮
L4398: , the moduli space of flat line bundles
L4401: on A. They showed that the corresponding functors set up an equivalence between the derived category of coherent sheaves on A♮ and the derived category of
L4402: D-modules on A.
L4404: Now, if A is the Jacobian variety Jac of a complex curve X, then A∨ ≃ Jac and A♮ ≃ Loc1, so we obtain the equivalence discussed above.
L4406: A slightly disconcerting feature of this construction, as compared to the original FourierMukai transform, is the apparent asymmetry between the two categories.
L4407:  But it turns out
L4410: | that this equivalence has a deformation in which this asymmetry disappears (see Sect. 6.3). |  |  |

L4642: 5. From GLn to other reductive groups
L4645: One adds a new dimension to the Langlands Program by considering arbitrary reductive groups instead of the group GLn. This is when some of the most beautiful
L4646: and mysterious aspects of the Program are revealed, such as the appearance of the Langlands dual group.
L4648: In this section we will trace the appearance of the dual group in the classical context and then talk about its geometrization/categorification.
L4650: 5.1. The spherical Hecke algebra for an arbitrary reductive group. Suppose we want to find an analogue of the Langlands correspondence from Theorem 1 where
L4651: instead of automorphic representations of GLn(A) we consider automorphic representations of G(A), where G is a connected reductive algebraic group over Fq. To
L4652: simplify our discussion, we will assume in what follows that G is also split over Fq, which means that G contains a split torus T of maximal rank (isomorphic to
L4653:  the direct product of copies of the multiplicative group).42 We wish to relate those representations to some data corresponding to the Galois group Gal(F/F),
L4654: the way we did for GLn. In the case of GLn this relation satisfies an important compatibility condition that the Hecke eigenvalues of an automorphic
L4655: representation
L4658: coincide with the Frobenius eigenvalues of the corresponding Galois representation. Now we need to find an analogue of this compatibility condition for general
L4659: reductive groups.
L4661: The first step is to understand the structure of the proper analogue of the spherical Hecke algebra Hx. For G = GLn we saw that this algebra is isomorphic to
L4662: the algebra of symmetric Laurent polynomials in_n variables. Now we need to give a similar description of
L4665: the analogue of this algebra Hx for a general reductive group G.
L4667: So let G be a connected reductive group over a finite field k which is split over k, and T a split maximal torus in G. Then we attach to this torus two
L4668: lattices, P and Pˇ, or

L4680:  equal to this number. The sets P and Pˇ contain subsets \Delta and \Delta∨ of roots and coroots of G, respectively (see, e.g., \[68\] for more details). Let

L4824:  The fact that f is compactly supported implies that the sum in the right hand side is finite. From this formula it is not at all obvious why this map should be
L4825:  a homomorphism of algebras. The proof is based on the usage of matrix elements of a particular class of induced representations of G(F), called the principal
L4826: series (see \[67\]).
L4828: The following result is referred to as the Satake isomorphism.
L4830: Theorem 4. The algebra homomorphism H → C[Pˇ] is injective and its image is equal to the subalgebra C[Pˇ]W of W-invariants, where W is the Weyl group of G.
L4832: A crucial observation of R. Langlands \[1\] was that C[Pˇ]W is nothing but the representation ring of a complex reductive group. But this group is not G(C)! The
L4833:  representation ring of G(C) is C[P]W , not C[Pˇ]W . Rather, it is the representation ring of the so-called Langlands dual group of G, which is usually denoted
L4834: by LG(C). By definition, LG(C) is the reductive group over C with a maximal torus LT(C) that is dual to T, so that the lattices of characters and cocharacters
L4835: of LT(C) are those of T interchanged. The sets of roots and coroots of LG(C) are by definition those of G, but also interchanged. By the general classification
L4836: of reductive groups over an algebraically closed field, this defines LG(C) uniquely up to an isomorphism (see \[68\]). For instance, the dual group of GLn is
L4837: again GLn, SLn is dual to PGLn, SO2n+1 is dual to Spn, and SO2n is self-dual.
L4839: At the level of Lie algebras, the Langlands duality changes the types of the simple factors of the Lie algebra of G by taking the transpose of the corresponding
L4840:  Cartan matrices.
L4842: Thus, only the simple factors of types B and C are affected (they get interchanged). But the duality is more subtle at the level of Lie groups, as there is
L4843: usually more than one Lie group attached to a given Lie algebra. For instance, if G is a connected simply-connected
L4846: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 59
L4849: simple Lie group, such as SLn, its Langlands dual group is a connected Lie group with the same Lie algebra, but it is of adjoint type (in this case, PGLn).
L4851: Let Rep LG be the Grothendieck ring of the category of finite-dimensional representations of LG(C). The lattice of characters of LG is Pˇ, and so we have the
L4852: character
L4855: homomorphism Rep LG → C\[Pˇ\]. It is injective and its image is equal to C\[Pˇ\]W . Therefore Theorem 4 may be interpreted as saying that H ≃ Rep LG(C). It
L4856: follows then that the
L4859: homomorphisms H → C are nothing but the semi-simple conjugacy classes of LG(C). Indeed, if γ is a semi-simple conjugacy class in LG(C), then we attach to it a
L4860: one-dimensional representation of Rep LG ≃ H by the formula [V ] 7→ Tr(γ,V ). This is the key step towards formulating the Langlands correspondence for
L4861: arbitrary reductive groups. Let us
L4864: summarize: Theorem 5. The spherical Hecke algebra H(G(F),G(O)) is isomorphic to the complexified representation ring Rep LG(C) where LG(C) is the Langlands dual
L4865:  group to G. There is a bijection between Spec H(G(F),G(O)), i.e., the set of homomorphisms H(G(F),G(O)) → C, and the set of semi-simple conjugacy classes in
L4866: LG(C).
L4868: \[
L4870: 5.3. The Langlands correspondence for an arbitrary reductive group. Now we can formulate for an arbitrary reductive group G an analogue of the compatibility
L4871: stateN ment in the Langlands correspondence Theorem_1 for GLn. Namely, suppose that \pi =
L4876: ### ′
L4879: x∈X πx is a cuspidal automorphic representation of G(A). For all but finitely many x ∈ X the representation πx of G(Fx) is unramified, i.e., the space of
L4880: G(Ox)-invariants in πx is non-zero. One shows that in this case the space of G(Ox)-invariants is onedimensional, generated by a non-zero vector vx, and Hx acts
L4881: on it by the formula fx · vx = φ(fx)vx, fx ∈ Hx, where φ is a homomorphism Hx → C. By Theorem_5, φ corresponds to a semi-simple conjugacy class γx in LG(C).
L4882: Thus, we attach to an automorphic representation a collection {γx} of semi-simple conjugacy classes in LG(C) for almost all points of X. For example, if G =
L4883: GLn, then a semi-simple conjugacy class γx in LGLn(C) = GLn(C) is the same as an unordered_n-tuple of non-zero complex numbers. In Sect. 2.3 we saw that such a
L4884: collection (z1(πx),... ,zn(πx)) indeed encoded the eigenvalues of the Hecke operators. Now we see that for a general group G the eigenvalues of the Hecke
L4885: algebra Hx are encoded by a semi-simple conjugacy class γx in the Langlands dual group LG(C).
L4887: Therefore on the other side of the Langlands correspondence we need some sort of Galois data which would also involve such conjugacy classes. Up to now we have
L4888: worked with complex valued functions on G(F), but when trying to formulate the global Langlands correspondence, we should replace C by Q\ell
L4891: , and in particular, consider the Langlands dual
L4894: group over Q\ell
L4897: , just as we did before for GLn (see the discussion after Theorem 1).
L4900: One candidate for the Galois parameters of automorphic representations that immediately comes to mind is a homomorphism
L4903: ### σ : Gal(F/F) → LG(Q\ell),
L4906: \[
L4908: which is almost everywhere unramified. Then we may attach to \sigma a collection of conjugacy classes {\sigma(Frx)} of LG(Q\ell) at almost all points x \in X,
L4909: and those are precisely the parameters of the irreducible unramified representations of the local factors G(Fx) of
L4911: \]
L4914: ### 60  EDWARD FRENKEL
L4917: \[
L4919: G(A), by the Satake isomorphism. Thus, if \sigma is everywhere unramified, we obtain for each x \in X an irreducible representation \pi x of G(Fx), and their
L4920: restricted tensor product is an irreducible representation of G(A) attached to \sigma, which we hope to be automorphic, in the appropriate sense.
L4922: \]
L4924: So in the first approximation we may formulate the Langlands correspondence for general reductive groups as a correspondence between automorphic representations
L4925:  of G(A)
L4928: and Galois homomorphisms Gal(F/F) → LG(Q\ell) which satisfies the following compatibility condition: if π corresponds to σ, then the LG-conjugacy classes
L4929: attached to π through
L4932: the action of the Hecke algebra are the same as the Frobenius LG-conjugacy classes attached to σ.
L4935: Unfortunately, the situation is not as clear-cut as in the case of GLn because many of the results which facilitate the Langlands correspondence for GLn are no
L4936: longer true in general. For instance, it is not true in general that the collection of the Hecke conjugacy classes determines the automorphic representation
L4937: uniquely or that the collection of
L4940: the Frobenius conjugacy classes determines the Galois representation uniquely. For this reason one expects that to a Galois representation corresponds not a
L4941: single automorphic representation but a finite set of those (an “L-packet” or an “A-packet”). Moreover, the multiplicities of automorphic representations in the
L4942:  space of functions on G(F)\\G(A) can now be greater than_1, unlike the case of GLn. Therefore even the statement of the Langlands conjecture becomes a much
L4943: more subtle issue for a general reductive group (see \[63\]).
L4946: However, the main idea appears to be correct: we expect that there is a relationship, still very mysterious, between automorphic representations of G(A) and
L4947: homomorphisms from the Galois group Gal(F/F) to the Langlands dual group LG.
L4949: We are not going to explore in this survey the subtle issues related to a more precise formulation of this relationship.44 Rather, in the hope of gaining some
L4950: insight into this mystery, we would like to formulate a geometric analogue of this relationship. The first step is to develop a geometric version of the Satake
L4951: isomorphism.
L4954: 5.4. Categorification of the spherical Hecke algebra. Let us look at the isomorphism of Theorem_4 more closely. It is useful to change our notation at this
L4955: point and denote the weight lattice of LG by P (that used to be Pˇ before) and the coweight lattice of LG by Pˇ (that used to be P before). Accordingly, we will
L4956:  denote the weights of LG by λ, etc., and not λˇ, etc., as before. We will again suppress the subscript x in our notation. As we saw in the previous section,
L4957: the spherical Hecke algebra H has a basis {cλ}λ∈P+

L4964: weights of LG. It consists of the classes [Vλ], where Vλ is the irreducible representation with highest weight λ. However, under the Satake isomorphism these
L4965: bases do not coincide! Instead, we have

L4995: Let us consider H as the algebra of functions on the quotient G(F)/G(O) which are left invariant with respect to G(O). We have learned in Sect. 3.3 that
L4996: “interesting” functions often have an interpretation as sheaves, via the Grothendieck fonctions-faisceaux dictionary. So it is natural to ask whether G(F)/G(O)
L4997: is the set of Fq-points of an algebraic
L5000: variety, and if so, whether Hλ is the function corresponding to a perverse sheaf on this variety. It turns out that this is indeed the case.
L5002: The quotient G(F)/G(O) is the set of points of an ind-scheme Gr over Fq called the affine Grassmannian associated to G. Let PG(O) be the category of
L5003: G(O)-equivariant perverse sheaves on Gr. This means that the restriction of an objects of PG(O) to each G(O)-orbit in Gr is locally constant. Because these
L5004: orbits are actually simply-connected, these restrictions will then necessarily be constant. For each λ ∈ P+ we have a finitedimensional G(O)-orbit Grλ = G(O) ·
L5005: λ(t)G(O) in Gr. Let Grλ be its closure in Gr. This is a finite-dimensional algebraic variety, usually singular, and it is easy to see that it is stratified by
L5006: the orbits Grµ, where µ ∈ P+ are such that µ ≤ λ with respect to the usual ordering on the set of weights. As we mentioned in Sect. 3.3, an irreducible perverse
L5007:  sheaf on a variety V is uniquely determined by its restriction to an open dense subset U ⊂ V , if it is non-zero (and in that case it is necessarily an
L5008: irreducible perverse sheaf on U). Let us take Grλ as V and Grλ as U. Then U is smooth and so the rank one constant sheaf on U, placed in cohomological degree −
L5009: dimC U = −2hρ,λ ˇ_i, is a perverse sheaf. Therefore there exists a unique, up to an isomorphism, irreducible perverse sheaf on Grλ whose restriction to Grλ is
L5010: this constant sheaf. The sheaf on Grλ is called the Goresky-MacPherson or intersection cohomology sheaf on Grλ. We will denote it by ICλ. This is quite a
L5011: remarkable complex of sheaves on Grλ. The cohomology of Grλ with coefficients in ICλ, the so-called intersection cohomology of Grλ, satisfies the Poincar´e
L5012: duality: Hi (Grλ,ICλ) ≃ H−i (Grλ,ICλ).46 If Grλ were a smooth variety, then ICλ would be just the constant sheaf placed in cohomological degree − dimC Grλ, and
L5013: so its cohomology would just be the ordinary cohomology of Grλ, shifted by dimC Grλ. A beautiful result (due to Goresky and MacPherson when V is defined over a
L5014: field of characteristic zero and to Beilinson, Bernstein and Deligne when V is defined over a finite field) is that a complex of sheaves satisfying the
L5015: Poincar´e duality property always exists on singular varieties, and it is unique (up to an isomorphism) if we require in addition that its restriction to any
L5016: smooth open subset (such as Grλ in our case) is a rank one constant sheaf. 45µ ≤ λ means that λ − µ can be written as a linear combination of simple roots with
L5017: non-negative integer coefficients 46the unusual normalization is due to the fact that we have shifted the cohomological degrees by dimC Grλ = 1 dimR Grλ
L5020: ### 62  EDWARD FRENKEL
L5023: The perverse sheaves ICλ are in fact all the irreducible objects of the category PG(O)
L5029: up to an isomorphism.47 Assigning to a perverse sheaf its “trace of Frobenius” function, as explained in Sect. 3.3, we obtain an identification between the
L5030: Grothendieck group of PG(O) and the algebra of G(O)-invariant functions on G(F)/G(O), i.e., the spherical Hecke algebra H. In that sense, PG(O) is a
L5031: categorification of the Hecke algebra. A remarkable fact is that the function Hλ in formula (5.2) is precisely equal to the function associated to the perverse
L5032: sheaf ICλ, up to a sign (−1)2hρ,λ ˇ_i
L5035: .
L5038: Now we can truly appreciate formula (5.2). Under the Satake isomorphism the classes of irreducible representations Vλ of LG do not go to the characteristic
L5039: functions cλ of the orbits, as one could naively expect. The reason is that those functions correspond to the constant sheaves on Grλ. The constant sheaf on Grλ
L5040:  (extended by zero to Grλ) is the wrong sheaf. The correct substitute for it, from the geometric perspective, is the irreducible perverse sheaf ICλ. The
L5041: corresponding function is then (−1)2hρ,λ ˇ iHλ, where Hλ is given by formula (5.2), and this is precisely the function that corresponds to Vλ under the Satake
L5042: correspondence. The coefficients aλµ appearing in Hλ also have a transparent geometric meaning: they | measure the dimensions of the stalk cohomologies of ICλ

L5155: 5.6. The geometric Satake equivalence. We have seen above that the Satake isomorphism may be interpreted as an isomorphism between the Grothendieck group of the
L5156: category PG(O) and the Grothendieck group of the category Rep LG of finite-dimensional representations of the Langlands dual group LG. Under this isomorphism
L5158: the irreducible perverse sheaf ICλ goes to the irreducible representation Vλ. This suggests that perhaps the Satake isomorphism may be elevated from the level
L5159: of Grothendieck groups to the level of categories. This is indeed true.
L5160: In fact, it is possible to define the structure of tensor category on PG(O) with the tensor product given by a convolution functor corresponding to the
L5162: convolution product (5.1) at the level of functions. The definition of this tensor product, which is due to Beilinson and Drinfeld (see \[69\]), is reminiscent
L5163: of the fusion product arising in conformal field theory.
L5165: It uses a remarkable geometric object, the Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannian Gr(2), which may be defined for any curve X. This Gr(2) fibers over X2
L5169: , but its fiber over (x,y) ∈ X2 ,
L5172: \[
L5174: where x_6= y, is isomorphic to Gr \times Gr, whereas the fiber over (x,x) \in X_2 is isomorphic to a single copy of Gr (see [20],Sect. 20.3,for a review of this
L5175:  construction). One can define in terms of Gr \tag{2}
L5177: \]
L5179: \[
L5181: the other ingredients necessary for the structure of tensor category on
L5186: ### PG(O)
L5189: , namely, the commutativity and associativity constraints (see \[69\]).
L5191: Then we have the following beautiful result. It has been conjectured by V. Drinfeld and proved in the most general setting by I. Mirkovi´c and K. Vilonen \[69\]
L5192:  (some important results in this direction were obtained earlier by V. Ginzburg \[70\] and G. Lusztig \[71\]).
L5194: Theorem 6. The tensor category PG(O) is equivalent to the tensor category Rep LG.
L5196: Moreover, the fiber functor from PG(O) to the category of vector spaces, corresponding to the forgetful functor on Rep LG, is just the global cohomology functor
L5197:  F 7→ L (Gr,F).

L5245: The above theorem should be viewed as a categorification of the Satake isomorphism of Theorem 4. We will now use it to define the notion of a Hecke eigensheaf
L5246: for an arbitrary reductive group and to formulate a geometric version of the Langlands correspondence.
L5249: 6. The geometric Langlands conjecture over C
L5252: From now on we will work exclusively with curves over C, even though the definition of the Hecke eigensheaves, for example, can be made for curves over the
L5253: finite field as well. In this section we will formulate the geometric Langlands conjecture for an arbitrary reductive group G over C. Once we do that, we will
L5254: be able to use methods of conformal field theory to try and establish this correspondence.

L5484: 6.2. Non-abelian Fourier-Mukai transform? In Sect. 4.4 we explained the connection between the geometric Langlands correspondence for the abelian group GL1 and
L5485: the
L5488: Fourier-Mukai transform (4.6) (in the context of D-modules, as proposed by Laumon and
L5491: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 67
L5494: Rothstein). In fact, the Fourier-Mukai transform may be viewed as a stronger version of the geometric Langlands correspondence in the abelian case in that it
L5495: assigns D-modules (more precisely, objects of the corresponding derived category) not just to individual rank one local systems on X (viewed as skyscraper
L5496: sheaves on the moduli space Loc1 of such local systems), but also to more arbitrary O-modules on Loc1. Moreover, this assignment is an equivalence of derived
L5497: categories, which may be viewed as a “spectral decomposition” of the derived category of D-modules on Jac. It is therefore natural to look for a similar
L5498: stronger version of the geometric Langlands correspondence for other reductive groups - a kind of non-abelian Fourier-Mukai transform. The discussion below
L5499: follows the ideas of Beilinson and Drinfeld.
L5501: Naively, we expect a non-abelian Fourier-Mukai transform to be an equivalence of derived categories
L5504: derived category of
L5507: (6.3)  derived category of
L5510: ### ←→ D-modules on Bun◦
L5513: ### O-modules on LocLG
L5516: G
L5519: where LocLG is the moduli stack of LG-local systems on X and Bun◦ G is the connected component of BunG. This equivalence should send the skyscraper sheaf on
L5520: LocLG supported at the local system E to the restriction to Bun◦
L5523: G of the Hecke eigensheaf AutE.
L5526: If this were true, it would mean that Hecke eigensheaves provide a good “basis” in the category of D-modules on Bun◦ G, just as flat line bundles provide a good
L5527:  “basis” in the category of D-modules on Jac.
L5529: Unfortunately, a precise formulation of such a correspondence, even as a conjecture, is not so clear because of various subtleties involved. One difficulty is
L5530: the structure of LocLG. Unlike the case of LG = GL1, when all local systems have the same groups of automorphisms (namely, C ×), for a general group LG the
L5531: groups of automorphisms are different for different local systems, and so LocLG is a complicated stack. For example, if LG is a simple Lie group of adjoint
L5532: type, then a generic local system has no automorphisms, while the group of automorphisms of the trivial local system E0 is isomorphic to LG. This has to be
L5533: reflected somehow in the structure of the corresponding Hecke eigensheaves. For a generic local system E we expect that there is only one, up to an isomorphism,
L5534:  irreducible Hecke eigensheaf with the eigenvalue E, and the category AutE of Hecke eigensheaves with this eigenvalue is equivalent to the category of vector
L5535: spaces. But the category AutE0 of Hecke eigensheaves with the eigenvalue E0 is non-trivial, and it carries an action of the group LG of symmetries of E0. Some
L5536: examples of Hecke eigensheaves with eigenvalue E0 that have been constructed are unbounded complexes of perverse sheaves (i.e., their cohomological degrees are
L5537: unbounded). The non-abelian Fourier-Mukai transform has to reflect both the stack structure of LocLG and the complicated structure of the categories of Hecke
L5538: eigensheaves such as these. In particular, it should presumably involve unbounded complexes and so the precise definition of the categories appearing in (6.3)
L5539: is unclear \[62\].
L5541: We may choose a slightly different perspective on the equivalence of categories (6.3)and ask about the existence of an analogue of the Poincar´e line bundle P
L5542: (see Sect. 4.4) in the non-abelian case. This would be a “universal” Hecke eigensheaf PG on LocLG × BunG which comprises the Hecke eigensheaves for individual
L5543: local systems. One can use such a sheaf as the “kernel” of the “integral transform” functors between the two categories
L5546: ### 68  EDWARD FRENKEL
L5549: (6.3), the way P was used in the abelian case. If Conjecture 1 were true, then it probably would not be difficult to construct such a sheaf on Locirr is the
L5552: ### LG × BunG, where Locirr LG
L5555: locus of irreducible LG-local systems. The main problem is how to extend it to the entire LocLG × BunG \[62\].

L5558: transform. Let us

L5562: case that was presented in Sect. 4.5. Namely, it is a functor from the category of O-modules supported on a certain affine subvariety i : OpLG(X) ֒→ LocLG,

L6163:  has not yet been established.52 In \[78\] some results concerning this equivalence in the formal neighborhood of the point λ = 0 are obtained.
L6166: 6.4. D-modules are D-branes? Derived categories of coherent O-modules on algebraic varieties have recently become staples of string theory, where objects of
L6167: these categories are viewed as examples of “D-branes”. Moreover, various equivalences involving these categories have been interpreted by physicists in terms of
L6168:  some sort of dualities of quantum field theories. For example, homological mirror symmetry proposed by Kontsevich has been interpreted as an equivalence of the
L6169:  categories of D-branes in two topological string theories, type A and type B, associated to a pair of mirror dual Calabi-Yau manifolds.
L6171: However, in the Langlands correspondence, and in particular in the Fourier-Mukai picture outlined in the previous section, we see the appearance of the
L6172: categories of D-modules
L6175: instead of (or alongside) categories of O-modules. Could D-modules also be interpreted as D-branes of some kind? An affirmative answer to this question is an
L6176: essential part of Witten’s proposal \[3\] relating S-duality in four-dimensional gauge theories and the Langlands
L6179: correspondence that was mentioned in the Introduction. Examples of “non-commutative” D-branes related to D-modules have also been considered in \[79\], and in
L6180: fact they are
L6183: 52These dual Hitchin fibrations (restricted to the open subsets of stable Higgs pairs in T ∗ Bun◦
L6186: ### G and
L6189: ### T ∗ Bun◦
L6192: LG) have been shown by T. Hausel and M. Thaddeus \[77\] to be an example of the Strominger-YauZaslow duality.
L6195: ### 72  EDWARD FRENKEL
L6198: closely related to the deformed Fourier-Mukai equivalence in the abelian case that we mentioned above.
L6200: We close this section with the following remark. We have looked above at the cotangent
L6203: ### bundle T ∗ Bun◦
L6206: G to Bun◦ G and the twisted cotangent bundle to BunG viewed as moduli
L6209: space LocG of flat holomorphic bundles on X. Both are algebraic stacks. But they contain large open dense subsets which are algebraic varieties. For example, in
L6210:  the case when G = GLn, these are the moduli space of stable Higgs pairs of rank_n and degree_0 and the moduli space of irreducible rank flat vector bundles of
L6211: rank_n. Both are smooth (quasi-projective) algebraic varieties. Though they are different as algebraic (or complex) varieties, the underlying real manifolds are
L6212:  diffeomorphic to each other. This is the socalled non-abelian Hodge theory diffeomorphism \[80\]. In fact, the underlying real manifold
L6215: is hyperk¨ahler, and the above two incarnations correspond to two particular choices of the complex structure. It is natural to ask what, if anything, this
L6216: hyperk¨ahler structure has to do with the Langlands correspondence, in which both of these algebraic varieties play such a prominent role. The answer to this
L6217: question is presently unknown.
L6220: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 73
L6223: ### Part III. Conformal field theory approach
L6226: We have now come to point where we can relate the geometric Langlands correspondence to two-dimensional conformal field theory and reveal some of the secrets of
L6227:  the Langlands correspondence. The reason why conformal field theory is useful in our enterprise is actually very simple: the problem that we are trying to
L6228: solve is how to attach to a flat LG-bundle E on X a D-module AutE on the moduli stack BunG of G-bundles on X, which is a Hecke eigensheaf with the eigenvalue E.
L6229:  Setting the Hecke condition aside for a moment, we ask: how can we possibly construct D-modules on BunG? The point is that conformal field theories with affine
L6230:  Lie algebra (or Kac-Moody) symmetry corresponding to the group G give us precisely what we need - D-modules on BunG (more precisely, twisted D-modules, as
L6231: explained below). These D-modules encode chiral correlation functions of the model and it turns out that Hecke eigensheaves may be obtained this way.
L6233: In this part of the survey I will recall this formalism and then apply it to a particular class of conformal field theories: namely, those where the affine
L6234: Kac-Moody algebra has critical level. As the result we will obtain the Beilinson-Drinfeld construction \[15\] of Hecke eigensheaves on BunG associated to
L6235: special LG-local systems on X called opers. Moreover, we will see that the Hecke operators may be interpreted in terms of the insertion of certain vertex
L6236: operators in the correlation functions of this conformal field theory.
L6239: 7. Conformal field theory with Kac-Moody symmetry
L6242: The D-modules on BunG arise in conformal field theories as the sheaves of conformal blocks, or the sheaves of coinvariants (the dual spaces to the spaces of
L6243: conformal blocks), as I will now explain. Throughout Part III of these notes, unless specified otherwise, G will denote a connected simply-connected simple Lie
L6244: group over C.
L6246: 7.1. Conformal blocks. The construction of the sheaves of conformal blocks (or coinvariants) is well-known in conformal field theory. For example, consider the

L6588: The way it is written, this formula seems to define a holomorphic connection on the bundle of conformal blocks and at the same time it states that the
L6589: correlation functions are horizontal sections with respect to this connection. However, there is a small caveat here: the right hand side of this formula is not
L6590:  well-defined, because T(z) transforms not as a quadratic differential, but as a projective connection (with the Schwarzian derivative term proportional to the
L6591: central change c of the model). Because of that, formula (7.5) only defines a projectively flat connection on the bundle of conformal blocks. The curvature of
L6592: this connection is proportional to the curvature of the determinant line bundle on Mg,n, with the coefficient of proportionality being the central change c.

L6660: right hand side of formula (7.6) depends only on \mu i and not on \xi_i
L6665: . Since T(z) transforms as a projective
L6668: connection on X, this connection is projectively flat (see \[20\], Ch. 17, for more details).
L6670: This is the same connection as the one given by formula (7.5).
L6672: The projectively flat connection on the bundle of conformal blocks of the WZW theory has been constructed by various methods in \[85, 86, 87, 88, 89\].
L6674: For a general conformal field theory the notion of conformal blocks is spelled out in \[20\], Sect. 9.2. Consider the case of a rational conformal field theory.
L6675:  Then the chiral algebra A has finitely many isomorphism classes of irreducible modules (and the corresponding category is semi-simple). Given a collection
L6676: M1,... ,Mn of irreducible modules over the chiral algebra, the corresponding space of conformal blocks CA(M1,... ,Mn) is defined as the space of linear
L6677: functionals on the tensor product Nn
L6680: ### i=1 Mi which are invariant under
L6683: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 77
L6686: the analogue of the Lie algebra gout corresponding to all chiral fields in the chiral algebra A (in the sense of \[20\]).56 This invariance condition
L6687: corresponds to the Ward identities of the theory.
L6689: If A is generated by some fields J a (z) (as is the case in the WZW model), then it is sufficient to impose the Ward identities corresponding to those fields
L6690: only. That is why in the case of WZW model we defined the space of conformal blocks as the space of goutinvariant functionals. These functionals automatically
L6691: satisfy the Ward identities with
L6694: respect to all other fields from the chiral algebra. For example, they satisfy the Ward identities for the stress tensor T(z) (given by the Segal-Sugawara
L6695: formula (8.3)), which we have used above in verifying that the connection defined by formula (7.6) is well-defined.
L6697: In a rational conformal field theory the spaces CA(M1,... ,Mn) are expected to be finite-dimensional (see, e.g., \[90\]), and as we vary (X,(xi)), they glue
L6698: into a vector bundle CA(M1,... ,Mn) on the moduli space Mg,n. It is equipped with a projectively flat connection defined as above (see \[20\] for more details).
L6699:  So the structure is very similar to that
L6702: of the WZW models.
L6704: Let us summarize: the correlation functions in a rational conformal field theory are interpreted as the squares of holomorphic sections of a vector bundle (of
L6705: coinvariants) on Mg,n, equipped with a projectively flat connection. The sheaf of sections of this bundle
L6708: ### 57
L6711: may be viewed as the simplest example of a twisted D-module on Mg,n.
L6713: If our conformal field theory is not rational, we can still define the spaces of conformal blocks CA(M1,... ,Mn) and coinvariants HA(M1,... ,Mn), but they may
L6714: not be finitedimensional. In the general case it is better to work with the spaces of coinvariants
L6717: ### HA(M1,... ,Mn), because the quotient of Nn
L6720: ### i=1 Mi (see formula (7.4)), it has discrete
L6723: topology even if it is infinite-dimensional, unlike its dual space of conformal blocks. These spaces form a sheaf of coinvariants on Mg,n, which has the
L6724: structure of a twisted D-module, even though in general it is not a vector bundle. This is explained in detail in \[20\].
L6726: Thus, the chiral sector of conformal field theory may be viewed as a factory for producing twisted D-modules on the moduli spaces of pointed curves. These are
L6727: the D-modules that physicists are usually concerned with.
L6729: But the point is that a very similar construction also gives us D-modules on the moduli spaces of bundles BunG for conformal field theories with Kac-Moody
L6730: symmetry corresponding to the group G.
L6733: 58 So from this point of view, the chiral sector of conformal
L6736: field theory with Kac-Moody symmetry is a factory for producing twisted D-modules on the moduli spaces of G-bundles. Since our goal is to find some way to
L6737: construct Hecke eigensheaves, which are D-modules on BunG, it is natural to try to utilize the output of this factory.

L6741: outlined above.

L6805: not invariant under the gauge transformations, but rather defines a section of a line bundle on the quotient of the space of all (0, 1)-connections by the
L6806: (complex group G-valued) gauge transformations. This space is precisely the moduli space of holomorphic structures on our (topologically trivial) G-bundle, and
L6807: hence it is just our moduli space BunG. From this point of view, the projectively flat connection on the bundle of conformal blocks comes from the formula for
L6808: the variation of the correlation function of the gauged WZW model under the action of infinitesimal gauge transformations on the space of anti-holomorphic
L6809: connections. This is explained in detail in \[83, 84\].
L6811: For us it will be more convenient to define this connection from a slightly different point of view. Just as the moduli space of curves is (infinitesimally)
L6812: uniformized by the Virasoro algebra, the moduli space BunG of G-bundles on X is locally (or infinitesimally) uniformized by the affine Kac-Moody algebra. In
L6813: fact, it is uniformized even globally by the corresponding Lie group, as we will see presently. Using this uniformization, we will write the connection
L6814: operators as in formula (7.6), except that we will replace the
L6817: a (z) of the affine Lie algebra. This derivation will
L6820: stress tensor T(z) by the currents J be more convenient for us because it also works for general conformal field theories with Kac-Moody symmetry, not only for
L6821: the WZW models.

L6824: be analyzed similarly. We will follow closely the discussion of \[20\], Ch. 18.To explain the Kac-Moody uniformization of BunG, we recall the Weil realization

L6833: double quotient G(F)\\G(A)/G(O). The proof is the same as in Lemma 2: any G-bundle on X may be trivialized on the complement of finitely many points. It can
L6834: also be trivialized on the formal discs around those points, and the corresponding transition functions give us an element of the ad\`elic group G(A) defined up
L6835:  to the right action of G(O) and left action of G(F).
L6837: For a general reductive Lie group G and a general G-bundle P the restriction of P to the complement of a single point x in X may be non-trivial. But if G is a
L6838: semi-simple Lie group, then it is trivial, according to a theorem of Harder. Hence we can trivialize P on X\\x and on the disc around x. Therefore our G-bundle
L6839: P may be represented by a single transition function on the punctured disc Dx around x. This transition function is an element of the loop group G((t)), where,
L6840: as before, t is a local coordinate at x. If we change our trivialization on Dx, this function will get multiplied on the right by an element of G[\[t]\], and if
L6841:  we change our trivialization on X\\x, it will get multiplied on the left by an element of Gout = {(X\\x) → G}. Thus, we find that the set of isomorphism
L6842: classes of G-bundles on X is in bijection with the double quotient Gout\\G((t))/G[\[t]\]. This is a “one-point” version of the Weil type ad\`elic uniformization
L6843:  given in Lemma_2. Furthermore, it follows from the results of \[96, 97\] that this identification is not only an isomorphism of the sets of points, but we

L6937: 7.4. Construction of twisted D-modules. Let us consider a more general situation.
L6939: Let_k ⊂ g be a pair consisting of a Lie algebra and its Lie subalgebra. Let K be the Lie group with the Lie algebra_k. The pair (g,K) is called a Harish-Chandra
L6940:  pair. 62 Let Z be a variety over C. A (g,K)-action on Z is the data of an action of g on Z (that is, a homomorphism α from g to the tangent sheaf ΘZ), together
L6941:  with an action of K on Z satisfying natural compatibility conditions. The homomorphism α gives rise to a homomorphism of OZ-modules a : g \otimes C OZ → ΘZ.
L6942: This map makes g \otimes OZ into a Lie algebroid (see \[75\] and \[20\], Sect. A.3.2). The action is called transitive if the map a (the “anchor map”) is
L6943: surjective. In this case ΘZ may be realized as the quotient g \otimes OZ/ Ker a.
L6945: For instance, let Z be the quotient H\\G, where G is a Lie group with the Lie algebra g and H is a subgroup of G. Then G acts transitively on H\\G on the right,
L6946:  and hence we obtain a transitive (g,K)-action on H\\G. Now let V be a (g,K)-module, which means that it is a representation of the Lie algebra g and, moreover,
L6947:  the action of k may be exponentiated to an action of K. Then the Lie algebroid g \otimes OH\\G acts on the sheaf V \otimes C OH\\G of sections of the trivial
L6948: vector bundle on H\\G with the fiber V .

L6954: Ker a · (V \otimes C OH\\G). Thus, \Delta(e V) is a DH\\G-module. The fiber of \Delta(e V) (considered as a OH\\G-module) at a point p \in H\\G is the quotient

L7256:  of Gout-invariant functionals on L0,k. By an analogue of the Borel-Weil-Bott theorem, the dual space to the vacuum representation L0,k is realized as the space
L7257:  of sections of a line bundle Le\otimes k on the quotient LU/U, which is nothing but the affine Grassmannian Gr = G((t))/G[\[t]\] discussed above, where G is
L7258: the complexification of U. Therefore the space of conformal blocks Cg(L0,k) is the space of global sections of the corresponding line bundle L \otimes k on
L7259: BunG, realized as the quotient (7.8) of Gr. We obtain that the space of conformal blocks
L7262: \otimes k ) of global
L7265: corresponding to the vacuum representation is realized as the space Γ(BunG, L
L7268: \otimes k over BunG.
L7271: sections of L
L7274: 66various integral powers of L may be constructed as determinant line bundles corresponding to representations of G, see \[20\], Sect. 18.1.2 and references
L7275: therein for more details 67The reason for the appearance of parabolic structures (i.e., reductions of the fibers of the G-bundle at the marked points to a Borel
L7276:  subgroup B of G) is that a general highest weight module is not a (bg, G[\[t]\])- module, but a (bg, I)-module, where I is the Iwahori subgroup of G((t)), the
L7277: preimage of B in G[\[t]\] under the homomorphism G[\[t]\] → G. For more on this, see Sect. 9.7.

L7283: \[

L7299: . Thus, the dual bundle \Delta(L0,k) of coinvariants is Γ(BunG, L . It has a canonical section φ whose values are the projections of the vacuum vector in L0,k
L7300: onto the spaces of coinvariants. This is the chiral partition function of the WZW model. The partition function is the square of length of this section
L7301: \|\|φ\|\|2 with respect to a hermitean inner product on \Delta(L0,k). Since the bundle \Delta(L0,k) of coinvariants in the WZW model is the tensor product L
L7302: \otimes k \otimes V , where L is the determinant line bundle on BunG and V is a vector space, we find that the dependence of \Delta(L0,k) on the BunG moduli is
L7303: only through the determinant line bundle L \otimes k
L7306: . However, despite this decoupling, it is still very useful to take into account
L7309: the dependence of the correlation functions in the WZW model on the moduli of bundles.
L7311: More precisely, we should combine the above two constructions and consider the sheaf of coinvariants on the combined moduli space of curves and bundles. Then
L7312: the variation along the moduli of curves is given in terms of the Segal-Sugawara stress tensor, which is quadratic in the Kac-Moody currents. Therefore we find
L7313: that the correlation functions satisfy a non-abelian version of the heat equation. These are the Knizhnik-ZamolodchikovBernard equations \[9, 93\].68 In
L7314: addition, the bundle of conformal blocks over BunG may
L7317: be used to define the hermitean inner product on the space of conformal blocks, see \[83\].
L7319: However, it would be misleading to think that L \otimes k \otimes V is the only possible twisted Dmodule that can arise from the data of a conformal field
L7320: theory with Kac-Moody symmetry.
L7323: There are more complicated examples of such D-modules which arise from other (perhaps, more esoteric) conformal field theories, some of which we will consider
L7324: in the next section.
L7326: We believe that this is an important point that up to now has not been fully appreciated in the physics literature.
L7328: It is instructive to illustrate this by an analogy with the Borel-Weil-Bott theorem. This theorem says that an irreducible finite-dimensional representation of
L7329: highest weight λ of a compact group U may be realized as the space of global holomorphic sections of a holomorphic line bundle O(λ) on the flag variety U/T,
L7330: where T is the maximal torus of U. Any representation of U is a direct sum of such irreducible representations, so based on that, one may conclude that the only
L7331:  interesting twisted D-modules on U/T are the sheaves of sections of the line bundles O(λ). But in fact, the space of global sections of any twisted D-module on
L7332:  the flag variety has a natural structure of a representation of the corresponding (complexified) Lie algebra g. Moreover, according to a theorem of A.
L7333: Beilinson and
L7336: J. Bernstein, the category of DO(λ)
L7341: -modules corresponding to a non-degenerate weight \lambda is equivalent to the category of g-modules with a fixed central character determined by \lambda . So
L7342: if one is interested in representations of the Lie algebra g, then there are a lot more interesting D-modules to go around. For example, the Verma modules, with
L7343:  respect to a particular Borel subalgebra b \subset g come from the D-modules of =delta-functions” supported at the point of the flag variety stabilized by b.
L7345: \]
L7347: Likewise, we have a Borel-Weil-Bott type theorem for the loop group LU of U: all irreducible representations of the central extension of LU of positive energy
L7348: may be realized
L7351: 68for an interpretation of these equations in the framework of the above construction of twisted Dmodules see \[74\]
L7354: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 85
L7357: as the duals of the spaces of global holomorphic sections of line bundles on the quotient LU/T, which is the affine analogue of U/T. This quotient is isomorphic
L7358:  to the quotient G((t))/I, where I is the Iwahori subgroup. The vacuum irreducible representation of a given level_k is realized as the dual space to the space
L7359: of sections of a line bundle Le\otimes k on the smaller quotient Gr = LU/U. This is the reason why the space of conformal blocks in the corresponding WZW theory
L7360:  (with one insertion) is the space of global sections of a line bundle on BunG, as we saw above.
L7362: But again, just as in the finite-dimensional case, it would be misleading to think that these line bundles on the affine Grassmannian and on BunG tell us the
L7363: whose story about twisted D-modules in this context. Indeed, the infinitesimal symmetries of our conformal field theories are generated by the corresponding Lie
L7364:  algebra, that is the affine Kac-Moody algebra bg (just as the Virasoro algebra generates the infinitesimal conformal transformations). The sheaves of
L7365: coinvariants corresponding to representations of bg that are not
L7368: necessarily integrable to the corresponding group Gb (but only integrable to its subgroup G\[\[t\]\]) give rise to more sophisticated D-modules on BunG, and
L7369: this one of the main points we wish to underscore in this survey. In the next section we will see that this way we can actually construct the sought-after Hecke
L7370:  eigensheaves.
L7373: 8. Conformal field theory at the critical level
L7376: In this section we apply the construction of the sheaves of coinvariants from conformal field theory to a particular class of representations of the affine
L7377: Kac-Moody algebra of
L7380: ### ∨, where h ∨ is the dual Coxeter number of g
L7383: critical level. The critical level is_k = −h (see \[100\]). Thus, we may think about these sheaves as encoding a chiral conformal field theory with Kac-Moody
L7384: symmetry of critical level. This conformal field theory is peculiar because it lacks the stress tensor (the Segal-Sugawara current becomes commutative at_k = −h
L7385:  ∨). As bizarre as this may sound, this cannot prevent us from constructing the corresponding sheaves of coinvariants on BunG. Indeed, as we explained in the
L7386: previous section, all we need to construct them is an action of bg. The stress tensor (and the action of the Virasoro algebra it generates) is needed in order
L7387: to construct sheaves of coinvariants on the moduli spaces of punctured curves (or on the combined moduli of curves and bundles), and this we will not be able
L7388:  do. But the Hecke eigensheaves that we wish to construct in the geometric Langlands correspondence are supposed to live on BunG, so this will be sufficient for
L7389:  our purposes.69 Before explaining all of this, we wish to indicate a simple reason why one should expect Hecke eigensheaves to have something to do with the
L7390: critical level. The Hecke eigensheaves that we will construct in this section, following Beilinson and Drinfeld, will be of the type discussed in Sect. 3.4:
L7391: they will correspond to systems of differential equations on BunG obtained from a large algebra of global commuting differential operators on it. However, one
L7392: can show that there are no global commuting differential operators on BunG, except for the constant functions. Hence we look at twisted global differential
L7393: operators acting
L7396: \otimes k introduced in the previous section. Suppose we find that for
L7399: on the line bundle L some value of k there is a large commutative algebra of differential operators acting on
L7402: ### L \otimes k
L7405: . Then the adjoint differential operators will be acting on the Serre dual line bundle
L7408: 69affine algebras at the critical level have also been considered recently by physicists, see \[101, 102\]

L8773: 8.6. T-duality and the appearance of the dual group. The crucial property that enabled us to make this identification is the fact that the kernels of two
L8774: screening operators coincide (for irrational values of the parameter). This has a nice interpretation from the point of view of the T-duality. Consider the free
L8775:  bosonic theory compactified on the circle of radius_1/ν (here we assume that ν is real and positive). The Hilbert space of this theory is the following module
L8776: over the tensor product of the chiral algebra \pi_0 and its anti-chiral counterpart \pi_0:

L8809: the two theory, compactified on the circles of radii ν and_1/ν, are equivalent. Under T-duality the electric and magnetic vertex operators are interchanged
L8810: (see, e.g., \[109\], Sect. 11.2, for more details). Now consider the deformation of this free field theory by the magnetic vertex operator (8.22). This operator
L8811:  is marginal (has dimension (1, 1)) with respect to the stress tensor Tν(z) given by formula (8.20). According to the general prescription of \[110\], the
L8812: chiral algebra of the deformed theory (in the first order of perturbation theory) is the kernel of the operator R Vν(z)dz on the chiral algebra of the free
L8813: theory, which for irrational ν_2 is \pi_0. As we saw above, this chiral algebra is the Virasoro chiral algebra generated by Tν(z).
L8815: On the other hand, consider the deformation of the T-dual theory by its magnetic operator. Under T-duality it becomes the electric vertex operator of the
L8816: original theory which is given by formula (8.21). Therefore the corresponding chiral algebra is the kernel
L8819: V−1/ν (z)dz on \pi_0 (for irrational ν_2 ). The isomorphism between the of the operator R kernels of the two operators obtained above means that the chiral
L8820: algebras of the two deformed theories are the same. Thus, we obtain an interpretation of this isomorphism from the point of view of the T-duality. It is this

L9315: Yet another way to think of the module Vχ is as follows. The Sugawara field S(z) defined by formula (8.2) is now central, and so in particular it is regular at
L9316: z = 0. Nothing can prevent us from setting it to be equal to a “c-number” power series v(z) ∈ C[\[z]\] as long as this v(z) transforms in the same way as S(z)
L9317: under changes of coordinates, so as not to break any symmetries of our theory. Since S(z) transforms as a projective connection, v(z) has to be a c-number
L9318: projective connection on D, and then we set S(z) = v(z). Of course, we should also take into account all corollaries of this identification, so, for example,
L9319: the field \partial_z S(z) should be identified with \partial_z v(z) and the field A(z)S(z) should be identified with A(z)v(z). This gives us a new chiral
L9320: algebra. As an slb_2-module, this is precisely Vχ.

L9472: Away from the critical level we take it for granted that on any (positive energy) bgmodule the action of bg automatically extends to an action of the
L9473: semi-direct product of
L9476: the Virasoro algebra and bg. The action of the Lie subalgebra Der O of the Virasoro algebra may then be exponentiated to an action of the group Aut O. The
L9477: reason is that away from the critical level we have the Segal-Sugawara current (8.3) which defines the action of the Virasoro algebra. But at the critical level
L9478:  this is no longer the case. So while the Lie algebra Der O and the group Aut O still act by symmetries on bg, these actions do not necessarily give rise to
L9479: actions on any given bg-module. This is the main difference between the categories of representations of bg at the critical level and away from it.
L9482: 9.2. Twisted D-modules attached to opers. Now to Vχ we wish associate a D′ −h∨ - module \Delta(Vχ) on BunG. What does this twisted D-module look like? At this
L9483: point we need to modify slightly the construction of the localization functor \Delta that we have used so far. In our construction we realized BunG as the
L9484: double quotient (7.8). This realization depends on the choice of a point x ∈ X and a local coordinate_t at x. We now would like to rephrase this in a way that
L9485: does not require us to choose_t. Let Fx be the completion of the field F of rational functions on X at the point x, and let Ox ⊂ Fx be the corresponding
L9486: completed local ring. If we choose a coordinate_t at x, we may identify Fx with C((t)) and Ox with C[\[t]\], but Fx and Ox are well-defined without any choices.
L9487:  So are the groups G(Ox) ⊂ G(Fx). Moreover, we have a natural embedding C[X\\x] ֒→ Fx and hence the embedding Gout = G(C[X\\x]) ֒→ G(Fx). We now realize BunG in
L9488:  a coordinate-independent way as (9.4) BunG = Gout\\G(Fx)/G(Ox).
L9491: With respect to this realization, the localization functor, which we will denote by \Delta x, assigns twisted D-modules on BunG to (bgx,G(Ox))-modules. Here bgx
L9492:  is the central extension of g(Fx) defined as in Sect. 7.1. Note that the central extension is defined using the
L9495: residue of one-form which is coordinate-independent operation. We define the bgx-module Vk(g)x as Indbgx Ck and z(g)x as the algebra of bgx-endomorphisms of

L9527: Finally, all of our hard work will pay off: the D-modules \Delta x(Vχx ) turn out to be the sought-after Hecke eigensheaves! This is neatly summarized in the
L9528: following theorem of A. Beilinson and V. Drinfeld, which shows that D-modules of coinvariants coming from the general machinery of CFT indeed produce Hecke
L9529: eigensheaves. Before stating it, we need to make a few remarks. First of all, we recall that our assumption is that G is a connected and simply-connected simple
L9530:  Lie group, and so LG is a Lie group of adjoint type. Second, as we mentioned at the beginning of Sect. 8, the line bundle L \otimes(−h ∨) is isomorphic to the
L9531: square root K1/2 of the canonical line bundle on BunG. This square root exists and is unique under our assumption on G (see \[15\]).

L9539: D-module on BunG. Finally, as explained at the end of Sect. 8.3, OpLg (X) is naturally identified with the space of all connections on the oper bundle FLG on X.

L10292: 9.5. Quantization of the Hitchin system. As the result of Theorem 10 we now have at our disposal the Hecke eigensheaves AutE on BunG associated to the LG-local
L10293: systems on X admitting an oper structure (such a structure, if exists, is unique). What do these
L10296: ### D-modules on BunG look like?
L10299: Beilinson and Drinfeld have given a beautiful realization of these D-modules as the D-modules associated to systems of differential equations on BunG (along the
L10300:  lines of Sect. 3.4). These D-modules can be viewed as generalizations of the Hecke eigensheaves constructed in Sect. 4.5 in the abelian case. In the abelian
L10301: case the role of the oper bundle on X is played by the trivial line bundle, and so abelian analogues of opers are connections on the trivial line bundle. For
L10302: such rank one local systems the construction of the Hecke eigensheaves can be phrased in particularly simple terms. This is the construction which Beilinson and
L10303:  Drinfeld have generalized to the non-abelian case.

L10523: It is important to note that the system (9.17) has singularities. We have analyzed a toy example of a system of differential equations with singularities in
L10524: Sect. 3.5 and we saw
L10527: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 113
L10530: that solutions of such systems in general have monodromies around the singular locus.
L10532: This is precisely what happens here. In fact, one finds from the construction that the “singular support” of the D-module \Delta χe is equal to the zero locus p
L10533:  −1 (0) of the Hitchin map p, which is called the global nilpotent cone [57, 114, 59, 15]. This means, roughly, that the singular locus of the system (9.17) is
L10534: the subset of BunG that consists of those bundles P which admit a Higgs field η ∈ H0 (X, g ∗ M \otimes Ω) that is everywhere nilpotent. For G = GLn Drinfeld
L10535: called the G-bundles in the complement of this locus “very stable” (see \[114\]). Thus, over the open subset of BunG of “very stable” G-bundles the system
L10536: (9.17)describes a vector bundle (whose rank is as predicted in \[59\], Sect. 6) with a projectively flat connection. But horizontal sections of this connection
L10537: have non-trivial monodromies around the singular locus.84 These horizontal sections may be viewed as the “automorphic functions” on BunG corresponding to the
L10538: oper χ. However, since they are multivalued and transcendental, we find it more convenient to describe the algebraic system of differential equations that these
L10539:  functions satisfy rather then the functions themselves. This system is nothing but the D-module \Delta χe. From the point of view of the conformal field theory
L10540:  definition of \Delta χe, as the sheaf of
L10543: ), the singular locus in BunG is distinguished by the property that
L10546: coinvariants \Delta x(Vχx the dimensions of the fibers of \Delta x(Vχx ) drop along this locus. As we saw above, these fibers are just the spaces of
L10547: coinvariants HP(Vχx ). Thus, from this point of view the nontrivial nature of the D-module \Delta χe is explained by fact that the dimension of the space
L10550: of coinvariants (or, equivalently, conformal blocks) depends on the underlying G-bundle P. This is the main difference between conformal field theory at the
L10551: critical level that gives us Hecke eigensheaves and the more traditional rational conformal field theories with Kac-Moody symmetry, such as the WZW models
L10552: discussed in Sect. 7.6, for which the dimension of the spaces of conformal blocks is constant over the entire moduli space BunG. The reason is that the
L10553: bg-modules that we use in WZW models are integrable, i.e., may be exponentiated to the Kac-Moody group Gb, whereas the bg-modules of critical level that we used
L10554:  may only be exponentiated to its subgroup G\[\[t\]\].

L10563: (X) to the category of D′

L10582: transform. Therefore we may think of it as a special case of a non-abelian generalization of the Fourier-Mukai transform discussed in Sect. 6.2 (twisted by K1/2
L10583:  along BunG).
L10586: 9.6. Generalization to other local systems. Theorem 10 gives us an explicit construction of Hecke eigensheaves on BunG as the sheaves of coinvariants
L10587: corresponding to a
L10590: “conformal field theory” at the critical level. The caveat is that these Hecke eigensheaves are assigned to LG-local systems of special kind, namely, Lg-opers
L10591: on the curve X. Those form a half-dimensional subspace in the moduli stack LocLG of all LG-local systems on
L10594: 84conjecturally, the connection has regular singularities on the singular locus
L10597: ### 114  EDWARD FRENKEL
L10600: X, namely, the space of all connections on a particular LG-bundle. Thus, this construction establishes the geometric Langlands correspondence only partially.
L10601: What about other
L10604: ### LG-local systems?
L10607: It turns out that the construction can be generalized to accommodate other local systems, with the downside being that this generalization introduces some
L10608: unwanted parameters
L10611: (basically, certain divisors on X) into the picture and so at the end of the day one
L10614: needs to check that the resulting Hecke eigensheaf is independent of those parameters. In what follows we briefly describe this construction, following
L10615: Beilinson and Drinfeld (unpublished). We recall that throughout this section we are under assumption that G is a
L10618: connected and simply-connected Lie group and so LG is a group of adjoint type.
L10620: From the point of view of conformal field theory this generalization is a very natural one: we simply consider sheaves of coinvariants with insertions of more
L10621: general vertex operators which are labeled by finite-dimensional representations of g.
L10623: Let (F, \nabla) be a general flat LG-bundle on a smooth projective complex curve X (equivalently, a LG-local system on X). In Sect. 8.3 we introduced the oper
L10624: bundle FLG on X. The space OpLG(X) is identified with the (affine) space of all connections on FLG, and for such pairs (FLG, \nabla) the construction presented
L10625: above gives us the desired Hecke eigensheaf with the eigenvalue (FLG, \nabla). Now suppose that we have an arbitrary LG-bundle F on X with a connection \nabla.
L10626: This connection does not admit a reduction FLB+ to the Borel subalgebra LB+ ⊂ LG on X that satisfies the oper condition formulated in Sect. 8.3. But one can
L10627: find such a reduction on the complement to a finite subset S of X. Moreover, it turns out that the degeneration of the oper condition at each point of S
L10628: corresponds to a dominant integral weight of g.
L10630: To explain this, recall that F may be trivialized over X\\x. Let us choose such a trivialization. Then a LB+-reduction of F\|X\\x is the same as a map (X\\x) →
L10631: LG/LB+.
L10633: \[
L10635: A reduction will satisfy the oper condition if its differential with respect to \nabla takes values in an open dense subset of a certain \ell -dimensional
L10636: distribution in the tangent bundle to LG/LB+ (see,e.g.,[115]). Such a reduction can certainly be found for the restriction of (F,\nabla) to the formal disc at
L10637: any point y \in X\\x. This implies that we can find such a reduction on the complement of finitely many points in X\\x.
L10339: \]
L10641: For example, if G = SL2, then LG/LB+ ≃ CP1
L10644: . Suppose that (F, \nabla) is the trivial local
L10647: system on X\\x. Then a LB+-reduction is just a map (X\\x) → CP1
L10650: , i.e., a meromorphic
L10653: function, and the oper condition means that its differential is nowhere vanishing. Clearly, any non-constant meromorphic function on X satisfies this condition
L10654: away from finitely many points of X.
L10656: Thus, we obtain a LB+-reduction of F away from a finite subset S of X, which satisfies the oper condition. Since the flag manifold LG/LB+ is proper, this
L10657: reduction extends to a LB+-reduction of F over the entire X. On the disc Dx near a point x ∈ S the connection
L10660: ### \nabla will have the form
