L10894: 9.7. Ramification and parabolic structures. Up to now we have exclusively considered Hecke eigensheaves on BunG with the eigenvalues being unramified LG-local
L10895: systems
L10898: on X. One may wonder whether the conformal field theory approach that we have used to construct the Hecke eigensheaves might be pushed further to help us
L10899: understand what the geometric Langlands correspondence should look like for LG-local systems that are ramified at finitely many points of X. This is indeed the
L10900: case as we will now explain, following the ideas of \[44\]. Let us first revisit the classical setting of the Langlands correspondence. Recall that a
L10901: representation πx of G(Fx) is called unramified if it contains a vector invariant under the subgroup G(Ox). The spherical Hecke algebra H(G(Fx),G(Ox)) acts on
L10902: the space of G(Ox)-invariant vectors in πx. The important fact is that H(G(Fx),G(Ox)) is a commutative algebra. Therefore its irreducible representations are
L10903: one-dimensional. That is why
L10906: an irreducible unramified representation has a one-dimensional space of G(Ox)-invariants which affords an irreducible representation of H(G(Fx),G(Ox)), or
L10907: equivalently, a homomorphism H(G(Fx),G(Ox)) → C. Such homomorphisms are referred to as characters
L10910: of H(G(Fx),G(Ox)). According to Theorem_5, these characters are parameterized by semi-simple conjugacy classes in LG. As the result, we obtain the Satake
L10911: correspondence which sets up a bijection between irreducible unramified representations of G(Fx) and semi-simple conjugacy classes in LG for each x ∈ X. Now,
L10912: given a collection (γx)x∈X of semi-simple conjugacy classes in LG, we obtain a collection of irreducible unramified representations πx of G(Fx) for all x ∈ X.
L10913: Taking their tensor product, we obtain an irreducible unramified representation π = N′ x∈X πx
L10916: of the ad\`elic group G(A). We then ask whether this representation is automorphic, i.e., whether it occurs in the appropriate space of functions on the
L10917: quotient G(F)\\G(A) (on which G(A) acts from the right). The Langlands conjecture predicts (roughly) that this happens when the conjugacy classes γx are the
L10918: images of the Frobenius conjugacy classes Frx in the Galois group Gal(F/F), under an unramified homomorphism Gal(F/F) → LG.
L10920: \[
L10922: Suppose that this is the case. Then, according to the Langlands conjecture, \pi is realized in the space of functions on G(F)\\G(A). But \pi contains a unique,
L10923: up to a scalar, spherical vector that is invariant under G(O) = Q
L10928: ### x∈X G(Ox). The spherical vector gives rise to a
L10931: function fπ on
L10934: #### (9.21)  G(F)\\G(A)/G(O),
L10937: \[
L10939: which is a Hecke eigenfunction. This function contains all information about \pi and so we replace \pi by f\pi . We then realize that \tag{9.21}
L10941: \]
L10945: is the set of points of BunG. This allows us
L10950: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 117
L10953: to reformulate the Langlands correspondence geometrically by replacing fπ with a Hecke eigensheaf on BunG.
L10955: This is what happens for the unramified homomorphisms σ : Gal(F/F) → LG. Now suppose that we are given a homomorphism σ that is ramified at finitely many points
L10956:  y1,... ,yn of X. Suppose that G = GLn and σ is irreducible, in which case the Langlands correspondence is proved for unramified as well as ramified Galois
L10957: representations (see Theorem_1). Then to such σ we can also attach an automorphic representation N′ x∈X πx, where πx is still unramified for x ∈ X\\{y1,...
L10958: ,yn}, but is not unramified at y1,... ,yn, i.e., the space of G(Oyi )-invariant vectors in πyi is zero. What is this πyi ?
L10960: The equivalence class of each πx is determined by the local Langlands correspondence, which, roughly speaking, relates equivalence classes of n-dimensional
L10961: representations of the local Galois group Gal(Fx/Fx) and equivalence classes of irreducible admissible representations of G(Fx).85 The point is that the local
L10962: Galois group Gal(Fx/Fx) may be realized
L10965: \[
L10967: as a subgroup of the global one Gal(F/F), up to conjugation, and so a representation \sigma of Gal(F/F) gives rise to an equivalence class of representations
L10968: \sigma x of Gal(Fx/Fx). To this \sigma x the local Langlands correspondence attaches an admissible irreducible representation \pi x of G(Fx). Schematically,
L10969: this is represented by the following diagram: global ←\to \pi = O ′\pi x
L10974: ### σ
L10977: ### x∈X
L10980: local ←→ πx.
L10983: ### σx
L10986: So πyi is a bona fide irreducible representation of G(Fyi ) attached to σyi
L10992: ### σyi
L10998: in ramified too, that is to say it has no no-zero G(Oyi )-invariant vectors. Therefore our representation π does not have a spherical vector. Hence we cannot
L10999: attach to π a function on G(F)\\G(A)/G(O) as we did before. What should we do? Suppose for simplicity that σ is ramified at a single point y ∈ X. The
L11000: irreducible representation πy attached to y is ramified, but it is still admissible, in the sense that the subspace of K-invariants in πy is finite-dimensional
L11001: for any open compact subgroup K. An example of such a subgroup is the maximal compact subgroup G(Oy), but by our

L11012: representations of the local Galois group

L11045: The problem is that H(G(Fy ),Iy) is non-commutative, and so its representations generically have dimension greater than 1.86
L11048: ### Iy
L11051: If π is automorphic, then the finite-dimensional space π
L11054: ### y , tensored with the onedimensional space of Q
L11057: ### πx embeds into the space of functions

L11066: ### x6=y
L11069: This space consists of eigenfunctions with respect to the (commutative) spherical Hecke algebras H(G(Fx),G(Ox)) for x_6= y (with eigenvalues determined by the
L11070: Satake correspondence), and it carries an action of the (non-commutative) affine Hecke algebra H(G(Fy ),Iy). In other words, there is not a unique (up to a
L11071: scalar) automorphic function associated to π, but there is a whole finite-dimensional vector space of such functions, and it is realized not on the double
L11072: quotient (9.21), but on (9.22). Now let us see how this plays out in the geometric setting. For an unramified LGlocal system E on X, the idea is to replace a
L11073: single cuspidal spherical function fπ on (9.21) corresponding to an unramified Galois representation σ by a single irreducible (on each component) perverse
L11074: Hecke eigensheaf on BunG with eigenvalue E. Since fπ was unique up to a scalar, our expectation is that such Hecke eigensheaf is also unique, up to isomorphism.
L11075:  Thus, we expect that the category of Hecke eigensheaves whose eigenvalue is an irreducible unramified local system which admits no automorphisms is equivalent
L11076: to the category of vector spaces. We are ready to consider the ramified case in the geometric setting. The analogue of a Galois representation tamely ramified
L11077: at a point y ∈ X in the context of complex curves is a local system E = (F, \nabla), where F a LG-bundle F on X with a connection \nabla that has regular
L11078: singularity at y and unipotent monodromy around y. What should the geometric Langlands correspondence attach to such E? It is clear that we need to find a
L11079: replacement for the finite-dimensional representation of H(G(Fy ),Iy) realized in the space of functions on (9.22). While (9.21) is the set of points of the
L11080: moduli stack BunG of G-bundles, the double quotient (9.22) is the set of points of the moduli space BunG,y of G-bundles with the parabolic structure at y; this
L11081: is a reduction of the fiber of a G-bundle at y to B ⊂ G.
L11083: Therefore a proper replacement is the category of Hecke eigensheaves on BunG,y. Since our LG-local system E is now ramified at the point y, the definition of
L11084: the Hecke functors and Hecke property given in Sect. 6.1 should be modified to account for this fact. Namely, the Hecke functors are now defined using the Hecke
L11085:  correspondences over X\\y (and not over X as before), and the Hecke condition (6.2) now involves not E, but E\|X\\y which is unramified.
L11087: We expect that there are as many irreducible Hecke eigensheaves on BunG,y with the eigenvalue E\|X\\y as the dimension of the corresponding representation of
L11088: H(G(Fy ),Iy) arising in the classical context. So we no longer speak of a particular irreducible Hecke eigensheaf (as we did in the unramified case), but of a
L11089: category AutE of such sheaves.
L11092: 86in the case of GLn, for any irreducible smooth representation πy of GLn(Fy) there exists a particular
L11095: K
L1098: \[
L11100: open compact subgroup K such that dim \pi_i = 1, but the significance of this fact for the geometric theory is presently unknown
L11102: \]

L11108: This category may be viewed as a “categorification” of the corresponding representation of the affine Hecke algebra H(G(Fy),Iy).
L11110: In fact, just like the spherical Hecke algebra, the affine Hecke algebra has a categorical version (discussed in Sect. 5.4), namely, the derived category of
L11111: Iy-equivariant perverse sheaves (or D-modules) on the affine flag variety G(Fy)/Iy. This category, which we denote by PIy
L11114: , is equipped with a convolution tensor product which is a categorical version
L11117: of the convolution product of Iy bi-invariant functions on G(Fy). However, in contrast to the categorification PG(O) of the spherical Hecke algebra (see Sect.
L11118: 5.4), this convolution
L11121: (PIy ). Nevertheless, this category “acts”, in the appropriate sense, on the derived category of the
L11124: product is not exact, so we are forced to work with the derived category Db
L11127: category of Hecke eigensheaves AutE. It is this “action” that replaces the action of the affine Hecke algebra on the corresponding space of functions on (9.22).
L11129: Finally, we want to mention one special case when the representation of the affine Hecke
L11132: ### Iy
L11135: algebra on π y is one-dimensional. In the geometric setting this corresponds to connections that have regular singularity at y with the monodromy being in the
L11136: regular unipotent conjugacy class in LG. According to \[44\], we expect that there is a unique irreducible Hecke eigensheaf whose eigenvalue is a local system
L11137: of this type.87 For G = GLn these eigensheaves have been constructed in \[117, 118\].
L11140: 9.8. Hecke eigensheaves for ramified local systems. All this fits very nicely in the formalism of localization functors at the critical level. We explain this
L11141: briefly following \[44\] where we refer the reader for more details.
L11143: Let us revisit once again how it worked in the unramified case. Suppose first that E is an unramified LG-local system that admits the structure of a Lg-oper χ
L11144: on X without singularities. Let χy be the restriction of this oper to the disc Dy. According to the isomorphism (9.5), we may view χy as a character of z(g)y
L11145: and hence of the center Z(bgy) of the completed enveloping algebra of bgy a the critical level. Let CG(Oy),χy be the category of (bgy,G(Oy))-modules such that
L11146: Z(bgy) acts according to the character χy. Then the localization functor \Delta y may be viewed as a functor from the category CG(Oy),χy to the category of
L11147: Hecke eigensheaves on BunG with the eigenvalue E.

L11150: object, namely, the bgy-module

L11251: This construction may be generalized to allow singularities of this type at finitely many points y1,... ,yn. The corresponding Hecke eigensheaves are then
L11252: D-modules on the moduli space of G-bundles on X with parabolic structures at y1,... ,yn. Non-trivial examples
L11255: of these Hecke eigensheaves arise already in genus zero. These sheaves were constructed explicitly in \[28\] (see also \[115, 116\]), and they are closely
L11256: related to the Gaudin integrable system (see \[119\] for a similar analysis in genus one).
L11258: In the language of conformal field theory this construction may be summarized as follows: we realize Hecke eigensheaves corresponding to local systems with
L11259: ramification by considering chiral correlation functions at the critical level with the insertion at the ramification points of “vertex operators” corresponding
L11260:  to some representations of bg. The type of ramification has to do with the type of highest weight condition that these vertex operators satisfy: no
L11261: ramification means that they are annihilated by g[\[t]\] (or, at least, g[\[t]\] acts on them through a finite-dimensional representation), “tame” ramification,
L11262:  in the sense described above, means that they are highest weight vectors of bgy in the usual
L11265: 88actually, there are now many such line bundles – they are parameterized by integral weights of G, but since at the end of the day we are going to “untwist”
L11266: our D-modules anyway, we will ignore this issue 89recall that Z(bgy) is isomorphic to Fun OpLg (D× (Dy) ⊂ OpLg (D×
L11271: y), so any \chi y \in nOpLg y) determines
L11276: a character of Z(bgy)
L11279: LECTURES ON THE LANGLANDS PROGRAM AND CONFORMAL FIELD THEORY 121
L11282: sense, and so on. The idea of inserting vertex operators at the points of ramification of our local system is of course very natural from the point of view of
L11283: CFT. For local systems with irregular singularities we should presumably insert vertex operators corresponding to even more complicated representations of bgy.
L11285: What can we learn from this story?
L11287: The first lesson is that in the context of general local systems the geometric Langlands correspondence is inherently categorical: we are dealing not with
L11288: individual Hecke
L11291: eigensheaves, but with categories of Hecke eigensheaves on moduli spaces of G-bundles on X with parabolic structures (or more general “level structures”). The
L11292: second lesson is that the emphasis now shifts to the study of local categories of bgy-modules, such as the
L11295: categories CG(Oy),χy and CIy,χy
L11298: . The localization functor gives us a direct link between
L11301: these local categories and the global categories of Hecke eigensheaves, and we can infer a lot of information about the global categories by studying the local
L11302: ones. This is a new phenomenon which does not have an analogue in the classical Langlands correspondence.
L11304: This point of view actually changes our whole perspective on representation theory of the affine Kac-Moody algebra bg. Initially, it would be quite tempting for
L11305:  us to believe that bg should be viewed as a kind of a replacement for the local group G(F), where F = Fq((t)), in the sense that in the geometric situation
L11306: representations of G(F) should be replaced by representations of bg. Then the tensor product of representations πx of G(Fx) over x ∈ X (or a subset of X) should
L11307:  be replaced by the tensor product of representations of bgx, and so on. But now we see that a single representation of G(F) should be replaced in the geometric
L11308:  context by a whole category of representations of bg. So a particular representation of bg, such as a module Vχ considered above, which is an object of this
L11309: category, corresponds not to a representation of G(F), but to a vector in such a representation. For instance, Vχ corresponds to the spherical vector as we have
L11310:  seen above. Likewise, the category CIy,χy appears to be the correct replacement for the vector subspace of Iy-invariants in a representation πy of G(Fy). In
L11311: retrospect, this does not look so outlandish, because the category of bg-modules itself may be viewed as a “representation” of the loop group G((t)). Indeed, we
L11312:  have the adjoint action of the group G((t)) on bg, and this action gives rise to an “action” of G((t)) on the category of bg-modules. So it is the loop group
L11313: G((t)) that replaces G(F) in the geometric context, while the affine Kac-Moody algebra bg of critical level appears as a tool for building categories equipped
L11314: with an action of G((t))! This point of view has been developed in \[44\], where various conjectures and results concerning these categories may be found. Thus,
L11315:  representation theory of affine Kac-Moody algebras and conformal field theory give us a rare glimpse into the magic world of geometric Langlands
L11316: correspondence.
